5.5 Optimization of sample volumes and added spike [SOURCE: Page_92]

We now explore the optimal range of sample volumes and added spike solution to ensure ac- [SOURCE: Page_92]

curate AMS measurements and minimize error magnification. Three conditions must be met to [SOURCE: Page_92]

obtain optimal measurements by ID-AMS. [SOURCE: Page_92]

In a diagram of the spike chlorine mass versus sample chlorine mass, (Desilets et al. (2006), Fi- [SOURCE: Page_93]

gure 2.11), the 35 Cl/37 Clm and 36 Cl/35 Clm values of the binary mixing are depicted. Condition 1 [SOURCE: Page_93]

is represented by the solid black line and is calculated such as (mClw + mCls ) = 2.5 mg. Condi- [SOURCE: Page_93]

tion 2 is represented by the two red solid lines with line 2a corresponding to the lower boundary [SOURCE: Page_93]

coloured dashed lines represent the condition (3), 36 Cl/35 Clm equals ten times 36 Cl/35 Cls , for 36 Cl/35 Clw varying between 90 and 900 10−15 at.at−1 . [SOURCE: Page_93]

Two examples with different 36 Cl/35 Clw samples have been chosen to illustrate the optimization [SOURCE: Page_93]

diagram. If the sample has a 36 Cl/35 Clw = 200.10−15 at.at−1 (dashed grey line) or higher, the [SOURCE: Page_93]

area where the three above-mentioned conditions are satisfied is the grey area B, while if the [SOURCE: Page_93]

sample has a 36 Cl/35 Clw = 100.10−15 at.at−1 (orange dashed line) or higher it corresponds to the [SOURCE: Page_93]

spotted area A. These two examples highlight the limiting characteristic of condition 3 which [SOURCE: Page_93]

reduces the size of the area satisfying the three conditions when the 36 Cl/35 Clw decreases. It [SOURCE: Page_93]

reaches a limiting 36 Cl/35 Clw when the 3 line overlays the 2a line. Due to the characteristics [SOURCE: Page_93]

of the spike used in this study, this limit is achieved for a 36 Cl/35 Clw = 90.10−15 at.at−1 . Water [SOURCE: Page_93]

samples with 36 Cl/35 Clw lower than this limiting ratio cannot be analyzed while satisfying the [SOURCE: Page_93]

three conditions. In this case, condition 3, that is 36 Cl/35 Clm ten times above the spike ratio, [SOURCE: Page_93]

should be preferred, rather than trying to comply with condition 2 (35 Cl/37 Clm between 10 and [SOURCE: Page_93]

used to calculate the optimum spike and sample chlorine masses for several 36 Cl/35 Clw . [SOURCE: Page_95]

These masses are represented Figure 2.11 by dashed coloured circles at the intersections of the [SOURCE: Page_95]

and using the relation V=m/C, the volume is represented as a function of the concentration (0 [SOURCE: Page_95]

5. Isotope Dilution - AMS technique for 36 Cl and Cl determination in low chlorine content waters, Chemical Geology [SOURCE: Page_96]

Solid red lines correspond to condition 2a matching 1 on the one hand, and 2b matching 1, on [SOURCE: Page_96]

the other hand. Color dashed lines correspond to the condition 3 matching 1 for two 36 Cl/35 Clw [SOURCE: Page_96]

water sample ratios. The same examples have been chosen to illustrate volume optimization. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Grey area B corresponds to the optimum area if a sample has a 36 Cl/35 Clw = 200.10−15 at.at−1 [SOURCE: Page_96]

or higher (dashed grey line). Spotted area A corresponds to the optimum area if a sample has a 36 Cl/35 Clw = 100.10−15 at.at−1 or higher (dashed orange line). [SOURCE: Page_96]

Within the defined range, Figure 2.11 (b) shows two different trends at low and high chlo- [SOURCE: Page_96]

rine concentrations. When the Cl concentration is above 4 mg.L−1 , both the volume and the 36 Cl/35 Clw have a similar trend independently from the sample concentration, implying that the [SOURCE: Page_96]

appropriate volume depends upon the 36 Cl/35 Clw . Because the optimal volume range is narrow, [SOURCE: Page_96]

the sample volume is a critical choice. An average volume of 100mL will ensure optimal mea- [SOURCE: Page_96]

sured ratio 35 Cl/37 Clm between 10 and 100. To summarize, this volume enables the analysis of [SOURCE: Page_96]

any sample with [Cl] > 4 mg.L−1 and with 36 Cl/35 Clw > 200.10−15 at.at−1 , or any sample with [SOURCE: Page_96]

[Cl] > 6 mg.L−1 and with 36 Cl/35 Clw > 100.10−15 at.at−1 . On the other hand, for samples with [SOURCE: Page_96]

low concentrations (< 4 mg.L−1 ), Cl concentration and 36 Cl/35 Clw show a similar trend, inde- [SOURCE: Page_96]

pendently from the sample volume, thus suggesting that the choice of the volume is not deter- [SOURCE: Page_96]

minant but rather dictated by the available volume for sampling. An average volume of 600 mL [SOURCE: Page_96]

seems relevant for very low concentration samples. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Finally, this graph demonstrates that, for a given Cl concentration there is a lower limit of the 36 Cl/35 Clw under which the three conditions required for analysis are not fulfilled. For example, [SOURCE: Page_96]

a water sample with a [Cl]< 0.6 mg.L−1 and 36 Cl/35 Clw <100.10−15 at.at−1 cannot be analyzed. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Furthermore, a water sample with a [Cl]< 0.1 mg.L−1 cannot be analyzed in any case. These [SOURCE: Page_96]

limiting values are dependent on the maximum volume sample that is chosen (1000mL in this [SOURCE: Page_96]

study). For these extreme cases, a higher volume must be used. [SOURCE: Page_96]

5.6 Low stable chlorine concentrations determined by ID-AMS [SOURCE: Page_96]

In this section, we focus on chlorine concentrations ranging from 0.1 to 10 mg.L−1 and explore [SOURCE: Page_96]

the capability of the ID-AMS technique to measure such low concentrations in water samples. [SOURCE: Page_96]

### 2. I NTÉRÊTS ET MÉTHODES D ’ ANALYSE DES TRACEURS δ18 O, δ2 H, 14 C ET 36 C L

To do so, gravimetric standards first obtained by precise weighing are measured by ID-AMS. [SOURCE: Page_97]

Gravimetric standards concentrations obtained by weighing [SOURCE: Page_97]

Experiments were conducted by analysis a set of gravimetric solutions that contain between [SOURCE: Page_97]

0.1 and 10 mgCl.L−1 . These solutions were obtained by diluting two different stock solutions [SOURCE: Page_97]

with ultra-pure water. Stock solution-1 was prepared by dissolving NaCl into ultra-pure wa- [SOURCE: Page_97]

ter with a Cl concentration of 212.35±0.06 mg.L−1 . As expected, this solution is devoid of 36 Cl [SOURCE: Page_97]

(36 Cl/35 Cl= 2.8x^10−16 at.at−1 ). Stock solution-2 was a natural groundwater sample, Guite (Table [SOURCE: Page_97]

2.1), with a concentration of chloride of (8.9±0.6 mg.L−1 (obtained by ID-AMS) and a 36 Cl/Cl ra- [SOURCE: Page_97]

tio of (257±210)x^10−15 at.at−1 . Solution-2 was used to investigate the potential role of coexisting [SOURCE: Page_97]

anions. Thirty-five dilute solutions have then been obtained by the addition of the appropriate [SOURCE: Page_97]

volume of ultra-pure water to the two stock solutions (5.8, Table 2.4), measured 20 times with [SOURCE: Page_97]

precise balances (10−4 g for volume < 100 mL, 10−3 g for volume < 500 mL, 10−2 g for volume [SOURCE: Page_97]

< 1000 mL). The uncertainties associated with each concentration are then calculated by the [SOURCE: Page_97]

propagation of weighing errors (Table 2.3). [SOURCE: Page_97]

Chlorine concentrations determined by ID-AMS [SOURCE: Page_97]

This set of solutions was then analyzed with the ID-AMS technique after the addition of the op- [SOURCE: Page_97]

timal amount of spike, using the following chemical procedure. In this study, all samples were [SOURCE: Page_97]

processed in batches of 10-15 samples. A chemistry blank was processed in each batch to esti- [SOURCE: Page_97]

mate potential chemical contaminations. Ultra-pure reagents were used to minimize contami- [SOURCE: Page_97]

nation with natural chlorine. The first step consisted in the addition of the optimal amount of [SOURCE: Page_97]

spike. Chlorine was extracted by precipitation of AgCl, which was then re-dissolved in aqueous [SOURCE: Page_97]

NH$_3$ . A saturated solution of BaNO$_3$ was added to allow sulphur precipitation as BaSO$_4$ . It was [SOURCE: Page_97]

then removed from the solution by 0.45 µm mesh filtration. This step is essential to eliminate the [SOURCE: Page_97]

isobaric interferences of 36 S on 36 Cl during AMS measurements and was repeated twice. Puri- [SOURCE: Page_97]

fied AgCl precipitates were obtained after re precipitation in nitric acid solution and three times [SOURCE: Page_97]

washing with ultra-pure water. Precipitates were finally dried at 80 ◦ C and pressed in 6.6mm [SOURCE: Page_97]

diameter Ni-target holders. All samples were analyzed at the AMS Facility ASTER. [SOURCE: Page_97]

precise calibration of the spike and low errors associated with the direct ratio measurements [SOURCE: Page_98]

(reproducibility=3%). As presented in the first part of this study, this is due to the chosen optimal [SOURCE: Page_98]

range of stable ratio between 10 and 100. [SOURCE: Page_98]

lized ratios all lie between 1.15 and 0.9, independently from the chlorine concentration. Espe- [SOURCE: Page_98]

### 2. I NTÉRÊTS ET MÉTHODES D ’ ANALYSE DES TRACEURS δ18 O, δ2 H, 14 C ET 36 C L

Contribution of Cl and 36 Cl determination to the understanding of hydrology of low [Cl] wa- [SOURCE: Page_101]

ters [SOURCE: Page_101]

The three groundwater samples (Dikwa, Mani, Guite, Table 2.1) which were presented here to [SOURCE: Page_101]

assess the reproducibility and precision of the ID-AMS analytical technique, were collected in [SOURCE: Page_101]

the frame of a wider-scale study regarding the hydrology of the endorheic Lake Chad Basin [SOURCE: Page_101]

(LCB). This broader study aims to investigate the water residence time as well as the relation- [SOURCE: Page_101]

ships within the LCB multi layered aquifer system (Bouchez et al., 2014, 2015a). It is noteworthy [SOURCE: Page_101]

that the precise determination of chlorine contents in ground and surface waters is essential [SOURCE: Page_101]

to constrain recharge rates as demonstrated by many studies relying on the chlorine mass ba- [SOURCE: Page_101]

lance, especially in arid systems (Edmunds and Gaye, 1994; Zhu et al., 2003). Previous works [SOURCE: Page_101]

in Central Africa (e.g. : in the Congo Basin Negrel et al. (1993), Niger Gaillardet et al. (1999), [SOURCE: Page_101]

Cameroun Viers et al. (1997), Mali Picouet et al. (2002)) suggest that the chlorine contents of [SOURCE: Page_101]

most rivers are low ([Cl]<5mg.L−1 ). In particular, the LCB being located in the semi-arid Sahe- [SOURCE: Page_101]

lian belt, in Central Africa (Schneider, 2004), far from the Cl oceanic source, surface waters and [SOURCE: Page_101]

precipitation have low chlorine contents ([Cl]<1mg.L−1 , Zairi (2008)). It is therefore a suitable [SOURCE: Page_101]

context to validate the applicability of the analytical method developed here. As the analyti- [SOURCE: Page_101]

cal basement of a broader study, the following conclusions can be drawn from the partial re- [SOURCE: Page_101]

sults presented here. 36 Cl/Cl values measured in the Quaternary aquifer (Mani : (202±9)x^10−15 [SOURCE: Page_101]

at.at−1 , Guite : (257±10)x^10−15 at.at−1 ) reflect modern atmospheric input of chlorine-36, thereby [SOURCE: Page_101]

indicating sub-modern recharge of the Quaternary aquifer system. Conversely, the 36 Cl/Cl ratio [SOURCE: Page_101]

measured in the water sample from the deep confined aquifer (Dikwa : (13±1)x^10−15 at.at−1 ) is [SOURCE: Page_101]

significantly lower than those from the Quaternary aquifer, reaching values close to the secu- [SOURCE: Page_101]

lar equilibrium observed in equivalent aquifer systems (Fabryka-Martin, 1988). This evidences [SOURCE: Page_101]

a long residence time, likely exceeding several half-lives of the 36 Cl, of groundwater in the deep [SOURCE: Page_101]

aquifer of the LCB. The presented analytical method combining the determination of 36 Cl and [SOURCE: Page_101]

low Cl contents opens new insights to improve our knowledge of the biogeochemical chlorine [SOURCE: Page_101]

cycle. Of broad interest for the geochemical community, chlorine is indeed used as a tracer of [SOURCE: Page_101]

geochemical and hydrological processes. In equatorial to sub-tropical environments it is pivotal [SOURCE: Page_101]

84 [SOURCE: Page_101]

5. Isotope Dilution - AMS technique for 36 Cl and Cl determination in low chlorine content waters, Chemical Geology [SOURCE: Page_102]

either to reconstruct modern recharge and paleorecharge (Petersen et al., 2014; Scanlon et al., [SOURCE: Page_102]

2006) or to determine atmospheric contribution in surface water composition in order to assess [SOURCE: Page_102]

chemical weathering rates (Gaillardet et al., 1999; Negrel et al., 1993; Picouet et al., 2002; Viers [SOURCE: Page_102]

et al., 1997)). Such applications justify the crucial need for precise determination of [Cl] and [SOURCE: Page_102]

[36 Cl] in low chlorine content waters that remain analytically challenging. [SOURCE: Page_102]

5.7 Summary and Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_102]

The applicability of the ID-AMS technique to determine simultaneously 36 Cl and Cl concentra- [SOURCE: Page_102]

tions in low chlorine water samples (0.1 to 10 mg.L−1 ) has been studied. Firstly, analytical errors [SOURCE: Page_102]

and reproducibility associated with the analysis of chlorine ratios at the French AMS national [SOURCE: Page_102]

facility ASTER have been precisely determined. The errors on Cl and 36 Cl contents were evalua- [SOURCE: Page_102]

ted at ±5% and ±7%, respectively. The robustness and reliability of this technique to measure [SOURCE: Page_102]

low chlorine concentrations has been proved by the measurements of gravimetric standards by [SOURCE: Page_102]

ID-AMS. This study not only enlarges the applicability of 36 Cl studies to fresh systems but also [SOURCE: Page_102]

provides a fieldwork sampling scheme for 36 Cl studies in fresh systems. Indeed, as the measure- [SOURCE: Page_102]

ment of the water conductivity on the field may lead to an estimate of the chlorine concentra- [SOURCE: Page_102]

tion, the optimum volume of sample to collect can be deduced from the optimization diagram. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Acknowledgments [SOURCE: Page_102]

This work benefits from the financial support from Andra (Agence nationale pour la gestion des [SOURCE: Page_102]

déchets radioactifs, France) and FFEM, France (Fond Français de l’Environnement Mondial) [SOURCE: Page_102]

and was supported by the Agence Nationale de la Recherche through the LABEX OT-Med [SOURCE: Page_102]

(Objectif Terre : Bassin Méditerranéen http ://www.otmed.fr/) and the project EQUIPEX ASTER- [SOURCE: Page_102]

### 1. Préambule

1 Préambule [SOURCE: Page_108]

Ce premier chapitre est présenté sous la forme d’un article qui va être soumis très prochaine- [SOURCE: Page_108]

ment. Dans cet article, la conceptualisation du lac en trois cuvettes distinctes, proposée par [SOURCE: Page_108]

Bader et al. (2011), est utilisée et complétée. Une inversion des données de hauteurs de lac [SOURCE: Page_108]

entre 1955 et 2011 permet de calculer le flux net de sortie sur chacune des cuvettes. Le lac étant [SOURCE: Page_108]

endoréique, ce flux correspond à la somme de l’évaporation (E), de la transpiration (T) et de [SOURCE: Page_108]

l’infiltration (I). Une modélisation isotopique et chimique est ensuite réalisée en se basant sur [SOURCE: Page_108]

le fait que seul l’évaporation est un processus fractionnant les isotopes stables de l’eau et que [SOURCE: Page_108]

seul l’infiltration est un processus qui élimine les sels du lac. Ainsi, la calibration du modèle [SOURCE: Page_108]

chimique et isotopique en utilisant les données existantes sur le lac permet de séparer les trois [SOURCE: Page_108]

termes du bilan hydrologique du lac (E, T, I). [SOURCE: Page_108]

Dans ce chapitre, nous montrons que la transpiration n’est pas négligeable puisqu’elle atteint 10 [SOURCE: Page_108]

à 15 % de l’évapotranspiration dans la cuvette sud et la cuvette nord et probablement jusqu’à [SOURCE: Page_108]

40 % dans la zone des Archipels. La régulation chimique du lac est assurée par une infiltration [SOURCE: Page_108]

de l’ordre de 10 % du flux total. La modélisation que nous avons développé permet de repro- [SOURCE: Page_108]

duire la transition d’un état de lac normal à un état de petit lac au cours des années 1970-1980. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Nous montrons que ces phases de réduction drastique de la surface du lac participent à une [SOURCE: Page_108]

évacuation efficace des sels vers l’aquifère quaternaire. [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 2. Hydrological, chemical and isotopic budgets of Lake Chad - abstract

2 Hydrological, chemical and isotopic budgets of Lake Chad - [SOURCE: Page_110]

abstract [SOURCE: Page_110]

Authors : Camille Bouchez, Julio Goncalves, Pierre Deschamps, Christine Vallet-Coulomb, Bruno [SOURCE: Page_110]

Hamelin, Jean-Claude Doumnang, Florence Sylvestre [SOURCE: Page_110]

In the Sahelian belt, Lake Chad is a key water body for 13 million people who live on its re- [SOURCE: Page_110]

sources. It experiences, however, substantial and frequent surface changes. Located at the cen- [SOURCE: Page_110]

ter of one of the largest endorheic basins in the world, its waters remain surprisingly fresh. Its [SOURCE: Page_110]

low salinity has been attributed to a low infiltration flow whose value remains poorly constrai- [SOURCE: Page_110]

ned. Understanding the lake’s hydrological behavior in response to climate variability requires a [SOURCE: Page_110]

better constraint of the factors that control its water and chemical balance. Based on the three- [SOURCE: Page_110]

pool conceptualization of Lake Chad proposed by Bader, J.C., Lemoalle, J. and Leblanc M. (2011) [SOURCE: Page_110]

[Hydrol. Sci. J., 56 : 3, 411-425], this study aims to quantify the total water outflow from the lake, [SOURCE: Page_110]

the respective proportions of evaporation (E), transpiration (T) and infiltration (I), and the asso- [SOURCE: Page_110]

ciated uncertainties. A Bayesian inversion method based on lake-level data was used, leading to [SOURCE: Page_110]

total water loss estimates in each pool (ETI). Sodium and stable isotope mass balances were then [SOURCE: Page_110]

used to separate total water losses into E, T and I components. Despite the scarcity of represen- [SOURCE: Page_110]

tative data available on the lake, the combination of these two geochemical tracers is relevant to [SOURCE: Page_110]

assess the relative contribution of these three outflows involved in the control of the hydrologi- [SOURCE: Page_110]

cal budget. Mean evapotranspiration rates were estimated at 2070±100 mm yr−1 and 2270±100 [SOURCE: Page_110]

mm yr−1 for the southern and northern pools respectively. Infiltration represents between 100 [SOURCE: Page_110]

and 300 mm yr−1 but most of the water is evapotranspirated in the first few kilometers from the [SOURCE: Page_110]

shorelines and does not efficiently recharge the Quaternary aquifer. Transpiration is shown to [SOURCE: Page_110]

be significant, around 300 mm yr−1 and reaches 500 mm yr−1 in the vegetated zone of the ar- [SOURCE: Page_110]

chipelagos. Hydrological and chemical simulations reproduce the marked hydrological change [SOURCE: Page_110]

between the "normal lake" state that occurred before 1972 and the "small lake" state after 1972 [SOURCE: Page_110]

when the lake surface shrunk to a tenth of its size. According to our model, shrinking phases are [SOURCE: Page_110]

efficient periods for salt evacuation from the lake towards the phreatic aquifer. [SOURCE: Page_110]

93 [SOURCE: Page_110]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

Key-words : Lake Chad ; infiltration ; transpiration ; hydrological and chemical model ; Bayesian [SOURCE: Page_111]

inversion [SOURCE: Page_111]

3 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_111]

The severe drought that affected the Sahel in the 1970s-1980s is one of the major signs of climate [SOURCE: Page_111]

variability recorded on a global scale during the XXt h century (Giorgi, 2002). The surface of Lake [SOURCE: Page_111]

Chad decreased from 21,000 km^2 in 1960 to 2,500 km^2 in 1987 (Lemoalle et al., 2014), illustra- [SOURCE: Page_111]

ting the consequences of climatic changes in the Sahelian belt (Ballatore and Muhandiki, 2002; [SOURCE: Page_111]

NASA Earth Observatory, 2001). Lake surface oscillations have major socio-economic impacts [SOURCE: Page_111]

on populations established along the lakeshores who depend on its fresh water and halieutic re- [SOURCE: Page_111]

sources for their livelihood (Hutchinson et al., 1992; Sarch, 2001; Sarch and Birkett, 2000). It was [SOURCE: Page_111]

initially thought that withdrawals of water, mainly for irrigation, contributed to the lake decline [SOURCE: Page_111]

observed during the 80s and 90s (Coe and Foley, 2001), but more recent studies have shown that [SOURCE: Page_111]

withdrawals were very likely overestimated and argue that extraction for irrigation is negligible [SOURCE: Page_111]

in the hydrological budget of the lake (Lemoalle et al., 2014). Since 2000, the lake surface area [SOURCE: Page_111]

has increased, reaching 14,000 km^2 in April 2013 due to more favorable rainfall in the western [SOURCE: Page_111]

Sahel (Lebel and Ali, 2009). This recent evolution of Lake Chad clearly indicates that its variabi- [SOURCE: Page_111]

lity is essentially driven by variations in the monsoon regime over its southern drainage basin, [SOURCE: Page_111]

combined with the very short residence time of the water in this system. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Although it is the outlet of an endorheic basin dominated by evaporation (~2000 mm yr−1 ) Lake [SOURCE: Page_111]

Chad waters remain surprisingly fresh (Carmouze, 1979; Roche, 1975). It has been suggested that [SOURCE: Page_111]

the chemical regulation of Lake Chad is controlled both by geochemical precipitation and clay [SOURCE: Page_111]

neoformation in the lake (Carmouze, 1983; Gac et al., 1977) and more significantly by infiltration [SOURCE: Page_111]

of the lake water into the underlying quaternary aquifer (Roche, 1975). The aquifer connected [SOURCE: Page_111]

with the lake is the major source of water for domestic use but its waters in some areas show [SOURCE: Page_111]

total dissolved salt concentrations above the recommended values. [SOURCE: Page_111]

A thorough estimate of the hydrological and chemical fluxes of Lake Chad is crucial to unders- [SOURCE: Page_111]

tand its variability and chemical regulation as well as to constrain the relationship between the [SOURCE: Page_111]

94 [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 3. Introduction

lake and the quaternary aquifer. Despite considerable efforts to evaluate evaporation and infil- [SOURCE: Page_112]

tration out of this lacustrine system, much remains to be done in order to better constrain the [SOURCE: Page_112]

hydrological and salinity budget of Lake Chad, especially in its present state, characterized by [SOURCE: Page_112]

seasonal or perennial pools. For instance, the impact of transpiration, which might be a signifi- [SOURCE: Page_112]

cant process impacting the hydrological budget owing to dense vegetation on the lake (Jasechko [SOURCE: Page_112]

et al., 2013), has never been taken into account so far. Different lake water balance models were [SOURCE: Page_112]

previously implemented to simulate fluctuations in the level of Lake Chad (Olivry et al., 1996; [SOURCE: Page_112]

Vuillaume, 1981). An integrated lake-catchment approach, coupling the distributed surface wa- [SOURCE: Page_112]

ter balance calculated over the basin (IBIS) with runoff transported across the land surface (HY- [SOURCE: Page_112]

DRA) described using a Digital Elevation Model (DEM) approach yielded a good representation [SOURCE: Page_112]

of the river discharge of the basin and the lake-levels in the eastern part (Coe and Foley, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Nevertheless, this model was unable to distinguish the different states and pools of the lake and [SOURCE: Page_112]

groundwater-surface water interactions were not accounted for. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Infiltration of lake waters into the Quaternary aquifer was evaluated by isotopic studies (Fontes [SOURCE: Page_112]

et al., 1970b; Gaultier, 2004; Zairi, 2008) or by quantitative approaches based on simple water or [SOURCE: Page_112]

salt budgets (Bader et al., 2011; Carmouze, 1972; Roche, 1980), direct estimates of seepage velo- [SOURCE: Page_112]

cities (Isihoro and Matisoff, 1990) or hydrogeological models (Isihoro et al., 1996; Leblanc, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_112]

However, they lead to a large range of infiltration estimates of between 2% and 32% of the total [SOURCE: Page_112]

inputs of the lake. The accurate quantification of leakage from lake waters is a challenging issue [SOURCE: Page_112]

especially in semi-arid to arid environments (Gee and Hillel, 1988), as the estimates are highly [SOURCE: Page_112]

dependent on the methods selected (LaBaugh et al., 1997). This suggests that a combination of [SOURCE: Page_112]

different approaches is necessary to accurately estimate all the fluxes of the lake water balance. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Chemical modeling using conservative elements has proved to be relevant to quantify ground- [SOURCE: Page_112]

water outflows (LaBaugh et al., 1997; Troin et al., 2010; Vallet-Coulomb et al., 2001). Isotopic [SOURCE: Page_112]

budgets have also been widely used to constrain lake water balances (Gibson, 2002b; Gibson [SOURCE: Page_112]

et al., 2002; Gonfiantini, 1986; Krabbenhoft et al., 1990; Sacks et al., 2013; Vallet-Coulomb et al., [SOURCE: Page_112]

2006). However, the combination of the two approaches has seldom been undertaken. [SOURCE: Page_112]

The objective of the present study is to improve the understanding of Lake Chad hydrology by [SOURCE: Page_112]

95 [SOURCE: Page_112]

The Lake Chad Basin (LCB) is a 2.5 billion km^2 hydrological closed drainage basin located in [SOURCE: Page_113]

central Africa, between Chad, Cameroon, Nigeria, Niger, and the Central African Republic. It is [SOURCE: Page_113]

bordered by high mountains, the Tibesti and the Hoggar in the North, the Amadaoua in the [SOURCE: Page_113]

96 [SOURCE: Page_113]

### 4. General description of the study site

South, the Aïr in the west and the Ennedi in the East, extending from 7◦ E to 24◦ E and from 5◦ N [SOURCE: Page_114]

to 25◦ N. The internal part is a flat sedimentary depression, with a mean topographic slope of [SOURCE: Page_114]

1.3 % (Le Coz et al., 2009). The drainage basin is characterized by a South to North climatic [SOURCE: Page_114]

gradient. The northern part lies in the Saharan climatic zone, dominated by low rainfall and [SOURCE: Page_114]

high evaporation rates. The southern Sudanese part is under a monsoon regime and is the only [SOURCE: Page_114]

hydrologically active part of the basin (about 610 000 km^2 ). Lake Chad is located in a small [SOURCE: Page_114]

depression in the central Sahelian part of the basin (Fig 3.1). [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.2 Lake morphology [SOURCE: Page_114]

The shallow lake is subdivided into three areas, the southern and the northern pools, separated [SOURCE: Page_114]

by an east-west vegetation-covered sand barrier named ‘the Great Barrier’, and an island area [SOURCE: Page_114]

in the east named the archipelagos (Fig. 3.1). These zones are either connected or disconnected [SOURCE: Page_114]

depending on the lake level. Different states and morphologies of the lake have been described [SOURCE: Page_114]

as a function of lake levels (Olivry et al., 1996). During the recent period on which this study [SOURCE: Page_114]

focuses, Lake Chad oscillated between its "normal" state, corresponding to a surface area of [SOURCE: Page_114]

about 20,000 km^2 occurring between 1950 and 1972 and its "small" state, below 15,000 km^2 [SOURCE: Page_114]

(Olivry et al., 1996) since 1972. Lake levels occasionally exceed a threshold located in the eastern [SOURCE: Page_114]

part of the southern pool, at 282.3 m, leading to overflows in the Bahr-el-Ghazal channel, which [SOURCE: Page_114]

drains water toward the Bodele depression (Fig. 3.1). During its “normal” state, the mean water [SOURCE: Page_114]

depth is about three meters in the southern pool and six meters in the northern pool. [SOURCE: Page_114]

4.3 Hydrological and chemical features of Lake Chad [SOURCE: Page_114]

The Chari-Logone River flows from the Central African Republic along the Cameroon border [SOURCE: Page_114]

into the southern shore of Lake Chad. It is the major tributary of the lake and accounts for [SOURCE: Page_114]

almost 80-90 % of the water inflow. Other major inputs are the Komodougou Yobe River, which [SOURCE: Page_114]

flows in Nigeria and Niger (5 %) into the northern pool and direct rainfall at the lake surface (5 to [SOURCE: Page_114]

10 %, Bader et al. (2011); Olivry et al. (1996). Lake Chad waters are subject to a high evaporation [SOURCE: Page_114]

rate of about 2000 mm yr−1 , which is the major water loss of the lake, while infiltration remains [SOURCE: Page_114]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

small. In the “normal” state, river discharge is around 40 km^3 yr−1 and rainfall at the surface [SOURCE: Page_115]

of the lake around 250 mm yr−1 (4 km^3 yr−1 ) for a lake volume of about 72 km^3 . Consequently, [SOURCE: Page_115]

under the rough assumption of a steady state, the residence time of the waters in the lake is very [SOURCE: Page_115]

short, about 1 to 2 years. With previous estimates of the annual infiltration into the Quaternary [SOURCE: Page_115]

aquifer of nearly 5 % of the lake volume, the residence time of a conservative element in the [SOURCE: Page_115]

“normal” state is around 20 to 40 years. Lake Chad hydrological budget is thus controlled by [SOURCE: Page_115]

the balance between rainfall in the southern part of the basin and evaporation over the lake [SOURCE: Page_115]

surface. This balance directly governs on very short time scales the volume but also the lake [SOURCE: Page_115]

surface because of the flat topography of the basin responsible for the lake shallowness. [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.4 The regional Quaternary aquifer [SOURCE: Page_115]

The lake is hydraulically connected to an unconfined aquifer of 500,000 km^2 in surface area [SOURCE: Page_115]

(Schneider and Wolff, 1992). It is composed by an alternation of silts, sands and clays resulting [SOURCE: Page_115]

from extended and restricted lacustrine phases during the Quaternary. The hydraulic gradient [SOURCE: Page_115]

and thus groundwater flow around the lake is oriented from the lake towards the aquifer except [SOURCE: Page_115]

at the North-East end of the lake (Fig. 3.1). The water table is characterized by three major pie- [SOURCE: Page_115]

zometric depressions around the lake, Chari-Baguirmi, Kadzell and Bornu and two piezometric [SOURCE: Page_115]

domes, Harr and Kanem (Fig. 3.1). The origin of such features, also described in other phreatic [SOURCE: Page_115]

aquifers in the Sahelian belt (Aranyossy and Ndiaye, 1993; Archambault, 1960), is still debated. [SOURCE: Page_115]

The main assumption of a high and localized evapotranspiration rate associated with low hy- [SOURCE: Page_115]

draulic conductivities was confirmed in the LCB by a steady state regional flow model of the [SOURCE: Page_115]

Quaternary aquifer (Boronina and Ramillien, 2008). Isotopic studies suggest that the main re- [SOURCE: Page_115]

charge occurred during past humid periods younger than 4000 years BP (Leblanc, 2002; Leduc [SOURCE: Page_115]

et al., 2000). Infiltration from both lake and rivers may also contribute locally to the recharge of [SOURCE: Page_115]

this unconfined aquifer but its quantification remains an issue (Djoret, 2000; Zairi, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_115]

### 5. General approach : combination of water, chemical and isotopic mass balances

5 General approach : combination of water, chemical and isoto- [SOURCE: Page_116]

pic mass balances [SOURCE: Page_116]

In the case of a shallow closed lake where aquatic vegetation has a non-negligible role through [SOURCE: Page_116]

transpiration fluxes, the hydrological, chemical and stable isotopic mass balances are expressed [SOURCE: Page_116]

as follows : ∆VL ³                                 ´ = S L × [P − (E + T + I)] + Qi n ∆t                                      t −∆t ∆(VL .CL ) ³                                   ´ = S L × [P.CP − I.CL ] + Qi n .Ci n                            (3.1) ∆t                                             t −∆t ∆(VL .δL )  ³                                               ´ = S L × [P.δP − (I + T).δL − E.δE ] + Qi n .δi n ∆t                                                          t −∆t [SOURCE: Page_116]

where VL is the volume of the lake (m^3 ), SL the surface (m^2 ), P the rainfall (m d−1 ), E the eva- [SOURCE: Page_116]

poration (m d−1 ), I the infiltration (m d−1 ), T the transpiration (m d−1 ), Qi n the river discharge [SOURCE: Page_116]

(m^3 d−1 ), CL, δL , CP , δP , Ci n and δi n respectively the concentrations (mg L−1 ) and isotopic com- [SOURCE: Page_116]

positions (‰) of the lake, the rainfall and the rivers, and δE the isotopic composition of the [SOURCE: Page_116]

evaporated moisture. This set of balance equations assumes a perfect mixing at each time step. [SOURCE: Page_116]

Considering a steady state of vegetation growth cycle in the lake, transpiration has no effect on [SOURCE: Page_116]

salt balance but has the same role as infiltration for the isotopic mass balance since it does not [SOURCE: Page_116]

induce any fractionation at stationary state (Welp et al., 2008). Therefore, in the chemical mass [SOURCE: Page_116]

balance, infiltration is the only process exporting salts while in the stable isotope mass balance, [SOURCE: Page_116]

evaporation is the only fractionating pathway. According to Craig and Gordon (1965), the isoto- [SOURCE: Page_116]

pic composition of the evaporated moisture above a lake can be calculated from the measured [SOURCE: Page_116]

relative humidity (h), the measured isotopic composition of the regional vapor (δA ), the isotopic [SOURCE: Page_116]

composition of the lake (δL ), the total fractionation coefficient (ε∗ ) and the kinetic fractionation [SOURCE: Page_116]

(εk ) coefficient as follows : (δL − ε∗ )/α − h.δA − εk δE =                                                      (3.2) 1 − h + εk [SOURCE: Page_116]

ε∗ = (α − 1) [SOURCE: Page_116]

εk = (1 − h).θ.n.CD                                (3.3) [SOURCE: Page_116]

99 [SOURCE: Page_116]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

where n, θ and CD are turbulence parameters such that n=1/2 for an average turbulent flow [SOURCE: Page_117]

(Gonfiantini, 1986), θ has been shown to have a value of 0.5 for the Eastern Mediterranean sea [SOURCE: Page_117]

(Gat, 1996) and CD was determined experimentally as CD18 O=28.5‰, CD$_2$ H=25.1‰(Merlivat, [SOURCE: Page_117]

1978). [SOURCE: Page_117]

According to the system of equations 3.1, E, T and I are undistinguishable in the water mass [SOURCE: Page_117]

balance but they are separated in the chemical and stable isotope mass balances. Denoting ETI [SOURCE: Page_117]

the sum of Evaporation, Transpiration and Infiltration, the exporting fraction of salts is defined [SOURCE: Page_117]

as FI =I/ETI and the fractionating fraction is FE =E/ETI. The equation can thus be rewritten as [SOURCE: Page_117]

follows : [SOURCE: Page_117]

∆VL ³                         ´ = S L × [P − ETI] + Qi n ∆t                              t −∆t ∆(VL .CL ) ³                                        ´ = S L × [P.CP − FI .ETI.CL ] + Qi n .Ci n                                 (3.4) ∆t                                                  t −∆t ∆(VL .δL ) ³                                                           ´ = S L × [P.δP − (1 − FE .ETI).δL − FE .ETI.δE ] + Qi n .δi n ∆t                                                                     t −∆t [SOURCE: Page_117]

According to the system of equations (3.4), ETI can be determined from the water mass balance, [SOURCE: Page_117]

the exporting salt fraction (FI ) i.e. infiltration from the chemical mass balance, and the fractio- [SOURCE: Page_117]

nating fraction (FE ) i.e. evaporation from the stable isotope mass balance. Therefore, the holistic [SOURCE: Page_117]

approach combining water, salt and isotope mass balances is a way to formally separate evapo- [SOURCE: Page_117]

ration, infiltration and transpiration occurring at the surface of the lake. The approach detailed [SOURCE: Page_117]

above for a single pool was applied to the three connected pools conceptualization of Lake Chad [SOURCE: Page_117]

(Bader et al., 2011). Water balances were calculated at a daily time step and total outflows (ETI) [SOURCE: Page_117]

were obtained from a Bayesian inversion of lake-levels. Chemical and isotopic mass balances [SOURCE: Page_117]

were then calculated at a daily time step using the parameters resulting from the inversion. The [SOURCE: Page_117]

FI and FE parameters were subsequently calibrated at each pool from the comparison between [SOURCE: Page_117]

observed and simulated chemical and isotopic values. Details are presented in Section 7. Accor- [SOURCE: Page_117]

ding to Equations (3.1)-(3.4), this modeling approach requires some input data (Qi n , Ci n , δi n , P, [SOURCE: Page_117]

CP , δP , δE ) as well as some calibration-validation data for comparison with simulation outputs [SOURCE: Page_117]

(hL , CL , δL ). [SOURCE: Page_117]

### 6. Hydrological, climatic and geochemical data

6 Hydrological, climatic and geochemical data [SOURCE: Page_118]

Our study is based on available hydrological, chemical and isotopic data and some additional [SOURCE: Page_118]

data acquired in this study on the LCB. The references and details on the data used are listed [SOURCE: Page_118]

in 11.1. The additional data acquired in this study are detailed in 11.2. A particular focus of [SOURCE: Page_118]

the study was the assessment of the reliability and the quantification of uncertainties of this [SOURCE: Page_118]

heterogeneous data set. [SOURCE: Page_118]

6.1 River data [SOURCE: Page_118]

The Chari-Logone discharges were reconstructed from river height measurements at the gauge [SOURCE: Page_118]

station of N’Djamena. The Komadougou Yobe and El Beid discharges were calculated from [SOURCE: Page_118]

observations and correlations with the Chari-Logone discharge (Bader et al., 2011). These di- [SOURCE: Page_118]

scharges were considered for a period between 1956 and 2011. Uncertainties which could de- [SOURCE: Page_118]

pend on installations, reading, gauging station calibration curve and reconstructions of the data [SOURCE: Page_118]

set were evaluated at a reasonable value of 10 to 15 % (Cogley, 1989). [SOURCE: Page_118]

The sodium concentrations and the isotopic compositions of the Chari-Logone and [SOURCE: Page_118]

Komadougou-Yobe Rivers were measured near their mouth between 1956 and 2009 (Carmouze, [SOURCE: Page_118]

1976; Djoret, 2000; Gac, 1980; Olivry et al., 1996; Roche, 1969; Zairi, 2008). We completed these [SOURCE: Page_118]

data sets by new data, from samples collected between 2008 and 2013. [SOURCE: Page_118]

The Komadougou-Yobe River is characteristic of more arid climatic conditions with a concen- [SOURCE: Page_118]

tration in Na+ between 0.2 and 0.5 mmol L−1 against 0.1 to 0.2 mmol L−1 in the Chari-Logone. [SOURCE: Page_118]

We observed the same trend with the oxygen isotopic composition which is between -4 ‰ and [SOURCE: Page_118]

+8 ‰ in the Komadougou Yobe against -6 ‰ and +3 ‰ in the Chari. Both [Na+ ] and δ18 O evi- [SOURCE: Page_118]

denced a high seasonality for the two rivers with minima recorded during high-water levels at [SOURCE: Page_118]

the end of the humid season (September) and maxima recorded during the dry season (April- [SOURCE: Page_118]

May). The long-term weighted average isotopic composition of the Chari Logone is -3 ‰ for [SOURCE: Page_118]

δ18 O and -20 ‰ for δ2 H. The inter-annual variability of concentrations and isotopic composi- [SOURCE: Page_118]

tions in rivers is low and there is no significant change in the water composition of the rivers [SOURCE: Page_118]

between the normal and small states of the lake (Fig. 3.2). [SOURCE: Page_118]

101 [SOURCE: Page_118]

6.2 Rainfall data [SOURCE: Page_119]

In this study, we used the rainfall dataset reconstructed by Bader et al. (2011) from precipitation [SOURCE: Page_119]

measurements at five stations around Lake Chad. Sodium concentrations in rainfall were mea- [SOURCE: Page_119]

sured at a monthly time step by Roche (1980) in 1969. They range between 0.05 and 0.24 mmol [SOURCE: Page_119]

L−1 . Higher concentrations, around 1 mmol L−1 were measured in rainfall in Nigeria (Goni et al., [SOURCE: Page_119]

2001) but are higher than the concentration of some river and lake samples. These questionable [SOURCE: Page_119]

values were thus not considered in this study. [SOURCE: Page_119]

Eighty-six non-continuous measurements of deuterium and oxygen isotopes in precipitation [SOURCE: Page_119]

were carried out between 1964 and 1995 at N’Djamena station (IAEA/WMO, 2015) (Fig. 3.3). The [SOURCE: Page_119]

measurements at N’Djamena define the local meteoritic line : δ2 H=6.3xδ18 O+4.3 (r^2 =0.95) with [SOURCE: Page_119]

long-term weighted averages and standard deviations of -3.8±1.7 ‰ for δ18 O and -18±10 ‰ for [SOURCE: Page_119]

δ2 H. The low slope of the local meteoritic line is in agreement with measurements throughout [SOURCE: Page_119]

6.3 Atmospheric vapor isotopic data, air temperature and relative humidity [SOURCE: Page_120]

The isotopic composition of atmospheric vapor is rarely measured and the current assumption [SOURCE: Page_120]

of an isotopic equilibrium with rainfall is highly questionable in climates with long dry periods [SOURCE: Page_120]

(Vallet-Coulomb et al., 2008). A few vapor measurements were performed in 1969 using the cryo- [SOURCE: Page_120]

genic trapping method (Fontes et al., 1970a), but because of their scarcity, and of the sampling [SOURCE: Page_120]

conditions, they can only be considered as a rough indication of the order of magnitude (Fig. [SOURCE: Page_120]

3.3). Recently, a detailed annual record of δ18 O of water vapor was performed by using laser [SOURCE: Page_120]

spectrometry at Niamey, Niger (Tremoy et al., 2012) (Fig. 3.3). It is the only available record in [SOURCE: Page_120]

the Sahelian band, and we assume that it can be used as a record of the seasonal isotopic va- [SOURCE: Page_120]

riations in the Lake Chad region, since both areas are under the same climatic conditions. This [SOURCE: Page_120]

assumption will be further discussed through sensitivity analysis. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Mean monthly data of air temperature and relative humidity were obtained from the Climatic [SOURCE: Page_120]

Research Unit (Harris et al., 2014) 0.5◦ x^0.5◦ data set. Air temperature reaches a maximum in [SOURCE: Page_120]

May (30 ◦ C) and a minimum in January (20 ◦ C) and relative humidity reaches a maximum in [SOURCE: Page_120]

August (70 %) and a minimum in Mars (20 %). The amplitude of variation and the low values of [SOURCE: Page_120]

6.4 Evaporation [SOURCE: Page_121]

Evaporation above Lake Chad was estimated by several methods. Measurements from lysimeters [SOURCE: Page_121]

provide the most reliable data while measurements by evaporimeter or evaporation pans lead [SOURCE: Page_121]

to different estimations (Monteith, 1991; Olivry et al., 1996; Riou and Dubois, 1967). Lysimeter [SOURCE: Page_121]

measurements at Bol (archipelagos) between 1965 and 1977 give an average annual evaporation [SOURCE: Page_121]

rate of 2170 mm yr−1 , a maximum during the dry season in April (229 mm month−1 ), another [SOURCE: Page_121]

one in October (199 mm month−1 ) and a minimum in January (136 mm month−1 ). The potential [SOURCE: Page_121]

evapotranspiration rates obtained from reanalysis by the CRU from 1900 to 2012 lead to a total [SOURCE: Page_121]

annual evaporation of 2022 mm yr−1 in the southern pool, 1952 mm yr−1 in the archipelagos [SOURCE: Page_121]

and 2365 mm yr−1 in the northern pool. [SOURCE: Page_121]

The different estimates remain within a 10 % error range, consistently with other studies (Ro- [SOURCE: Page_121]

senberry et al., 2007; Vallet-Coulomb et al., 2001), and the seasonality, the interannual variability [SOURCE: Page_121]

and the spatial variability were found to be similar whatever the method used. The seasonality [SOURCE: Page_121]

shown by the lysimeter measurements is significant and reveals the same pattern as the CRU [SOURCE: Page_121]

data. The interannual variability of the total evaporation is less than 10% (Olivry et al., 1996) [SOURCE: Page_121]

and the standard deviation on the CRU evaporation over the whole period is less than 2 % for [SOURCE: Page_121]

all pools (with a maximum in 2009 of 2100 mm yr−1 in the southern pool and a minimum in [SOURCE: Page_121]

1961 of 1941 mm yr−1 ). Moreover, no significant change during the climatic transition period [SOURCE: Page_121]

was observed. Finally, both methods show that evaporation rates are almost similar in the sou- [SOURCE: Page_121]

thern pool and in the archipelagos and 10 % higher in the northern pool. [SOURCE: Page_121]

6.5 Ground-based and altimetric lake level data [SOURCE: Page_121]

Lake-level was monitored non-continuously from 1956 to 2008 at three gauges, in Bol (archi- [SOURCE: Page_121]

pelagos), Kalom and Kirenowa (southern pool) and Nguigmi (northern pool), see Fig. 3.1 for [SOURCE: Page_121]

locations (Bader et al., 2011; Lemoalle, 2004; Olivry et al., 1996). The Bol station, located in the [SOURCE: Page_121]

### 6. Hydrological, climatic and geochemical data

archipelagos, provides the most complete record on a daily time scale. The other stations pro- [SOURCE: Page_122]

vide sparse observations. The data set comprises 885 lake-level data measured in the archipe- [SOURCE: Page_122]

lagos, 150 in the southern pool and 97 in the northern pool over the 1956-2008 period. Data in [SOURCE: Page_122]

the northern pool were completed by lake surface estimates obtained from remote sensing (Le- [SOURCE: Page_122]

6.6 Lake Chad chemical and isotopic data [SOURCE: Page_122]

Data from Carmouze (1976); Chantraine (1977, 1978); Chantraine and Lemoalle (1976a,b); [SOURCE: Page_122]

Gac (1980); Gaultier (2004); Lemoalle (1979); Maglione (1976); Roche (1980); Zairi (2008) were [SOURCE: Page_122]

collected and completed by new samplings performed between 2008 and 2012. Due to its endo- [SOURCE: Page_122]

rheism and climate characteristics, the lake is expected to be a concentrating basin of dissolved [SOURCE: Page_122]

salts. However, the water salinity is low and relatively time constant. Exhaustive studies carried [SOURCE: Page_122]

out by Carmouze (1976) on the whole lake during its “normal” state, between 1968 and 1971, [SOURCE: Page_122]

provide a description of its geochemical features. These data show a global trend of increasing [SOURCE: Page_122]

concentrations from the Chari-Logone delta (σ = 50 µS cm−1 ) to the northern pool (σ = 1000 [SOURCE: Page_122]

µS cm−1 ) and to a lower extent to the archipelagos (σ = 600 µS cm−1 ). The lake Chad waters 2− are dominated by HCO−         − 3 while Cl and SO$_4$ accounts for only 2 % of the anionic balance [SOURCE: Page_122]

with chlorine concentrations below 1mg L−1 . Calcium is the most abundant cation near the [SOURCE: Page_122]

Chari-Logone mouth, while sodium becomes dominant in more concentrated waters because [SOURCE: Page_122]

it does not react with the lake substratum or the vegetation (Carmouze, 1976). Therefore, [SOURCE: Page_122]

sodium and chlorine can be considered conservative elements except in the northern margin [SOURCE: Page_122]

where limited deposits of natron (Na$_2$ CO$_3$ ) are described. However, because of the low chlorine [SOURCE: Page_122]

content in the waters, very few accurate chlorine measurements are available. Hence, only [SOURCE: Page_122]

sodium is considered a conservative element in this study. Sodium concentrations in the lake [SOURCE: Page_122]

during the “normal” state were about 0.3 mmol L−1 in the southern pool, 2 mmol L−1 in the [SOURCE: Page_122]

northern pool and 0.5 mmol L−1 in the archipelagos (Fig. 3.4). [SOURCE: Page_122]

### 7. Methodology

7 Methodology [SOURCE: Page_124]

7.1 Conceptualization of Lake Chad (from Bader et al. (2011)) [SOURCE: Page_124]

Different lake water balance models were previously implemented to simulate the Lake Chad [SOURCE: Page_124]

level fluctuations (Olivry et al., 1996; Vuillaume, 1981). More recently Bader et al. (2011) pro- [SOURCE: Page_124]

posed a conceptualization of the lake comprising three interconnected pools, allowing a more [SOURCE: Page_124]

reliable and realistic description of the Lake Chad system. The model is thoroughly described in [SOURCE: Page_124]

Bader et al. (2011) and has the following main features. The lake is represented by three pools, [SOURCE: Page_124]

corresponding to the above-described northern and southern pools and archipelagos (Fig. 3.1). [SOURCE: Page_124]

The southern and northern pools, on the one hand, and the southern pool and archipelagos [SOURCE: Page_124]

on the other hand, are considered to be connected through channels. Flows into these chan- [SOURCE: Page_124]

nels are described according to the Manning-Strickler equation that provides an estimate of [SOURCE: Page_124]

average water flows in open channels. The Manning-Strickler parameter is a function of the [SOURCE: Page_124]

channel roughness but because of growing vegetation during low water states in Lake Chad, [SOURCE: Page_124]

this parameter is directly related to the water level. Consequently, a linear regression between [SOURCE: Page_124]

the Manning-Strickler parameter and the water level is considered, with A$_0$ (m^5/6 s−1 ) being the [SOURCE: Page_124]

intercept and A$_1$ (m−1/6 s−1 ) the slope of the regression. The two parameter sets are denoted [SOURCE: Page_124]

(A0SA , A1SA ) and (A0SN , A1SN ) for the channels between the southern pool and the archipelago [SOURCE: Page_124]

and the southern pool and the northern pool, respectively. Parameterization of the northern [SOURCE: Page_124]

pool water budget also involves a soil reservoir whose depth, denoted as ZR (m), is an unk- [SOURCE: Page_124]

nown parameter of the model. This parameter is essential to account for the presence of water [SOURCE: Page_124]

in the soil porosity when the northern pool dries up. Five additional parameters (A0SA , A1SA , [SOURCE: Page_124]

A0SN , A1SN , ZR), as compared to the one-pool conceptual model of Section 5, are thus intro- [SOURCE: Page_124]

duced. The surface-lake level relationships in the southern and northern pools were obtained [SOURCE: Page_124]

from bathymetric surveys. The surface of the archipelagos is considered to cover one fourth of [SOURCE: Page_124]

the surface of the southern pool and its surface-lake level relationship includes one unknown [SOURCE: Page_124]

parameter calibrated by Bader et al. (2011) and is kept unchanged in this study. With respect to [SOURCE: Page_124]

the challenge of modeling a complex lake system in a semi-arid environment and the scarcity [SOURCE: Page_124]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

of the available data, this conceptualization of Lake Chad hydrology provides a first order tool [SOURCE: Page_125]

for water management (Lemoalle et al., 2012). Based on a deterministic calibration of the lake [SOURCE: Page_125]

model, Bader et al. (2011) quantified infiltration using a forcing value of evaporation correspon- [SOURCE: Page_125]

ding to measurements. However, the estimated infiltration lies within the range of evaporation [SOURCE: Page_125]

uncertainty (10 %). Hence, a small error on the evaporation value introduced in the model will [SOURCE: Page_125]

have a major impact on the calibrated infiltration. Moreover, despite the density of the vegeta- [SOURCE: Page_125]

tion cover in some areas of the lake, the contribution of plant transpiration to the water and salt [SOURCE: Page_125]

balances of the lake was suggested (Lemoalle, 1979) but never quantified to our knowledge. Our [SOURCE: Page_125]

alternative approach proposes the following improvements : [SOURCE: Page_125]

• An evaluation of the uncertainties associated with input variables and modeling, an im- [SOURCE: Page_125]

portant issue widely discussed in the literature (Beven, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_125]

• An integration of water, chemical and isotopic mass balances in order to better constrain [SOURCE: Page_125]

poorly known terms of the lake water balance such as the respective contributions of eva- [SOURCE: Page_125]

poration, transpiration and infiltration to the lake water budget. We thus propose to go [SOURCE: Page_125]

one step further by using all the geochemical data available since 1950 in a holistic ap- [SOURCE: Page_125]

proach combining water, salt and isotope mass balances, as described in section 5. [SOURCE: Page_125]

### 7. Methodology

pool, then CS/A =CA and δS/A = δA . The isotopic composition of the evaporated moisture above [SOURCE: Page_128]

each lake pool was calculated from Craig and Gordon (1965) (Section 5). A threshold of sodium [SOURCE: Page_128]

concentration was added corresponding to the solubility of natron (Na$_2$ CO$_3$ ) according to the [SOURCE: Page_128]

drying episodes that occurred in the archipelagos and in the northern pool followed by the de- [SOURCE: Page_128]

position of natron evaporites. We consider that salts are removed from the lake system following [SOURCE: Page_128]

previous assertions of a rapid re-dissolution of salts by rainfall into the quaternary aquifer and [SOURCE: Page_128]

by exportation by winds (Fontes et al., 1969). [SOURCE: Page_128]

Data processing to obtain daily input variables for the model [SOURCE: Page_128]

Monthly-averaged sodium and isotopic compositions were calculated using the mean of indi- [SOURCE: Page_128]

vidual measurements carried out during each month of the whole time period. These one-year [SOURCE: Page_128]

monthly average values were used to reconstruct the evolution of river sodium concentrations [SOURCE: Page_128]

and isotopic composition from 1956 to 2011 : they were first downscaled to daily values by a [SOURCE: Page_128]

spline interpolation and then repeatedly used every year (Fig. 3.2, 3.3). Similarly, the one-year [SOURCE: Page_128]

monthly sodium concentrations in rainfall and isotopic compositions of vapor are considered [SOURCE: Page_128]

representative of the yearly evolution throughout the time period. Thus, these monthly records [SOURCE: Page_128]

were also downscaled to daily values by a spline interpolation and then repeatedly applied every [SOURCE: Page_128]

year (Fig. 3.2, 3.3). Temperature and relative humidity data is available on a monthly time scale [SOURCE: Page_128]

for the entire 1956-2011 time period. The isotopic compositions of rainfall are available on a [SOURCE: Page_128]

monthly time scale between 1964 and 1978 and missing monthly data was reconstructed using [SOURCE: Page_128]

the mean value of data for the same month. A spline interpolation was used to reconstruct daily [SOURCE: Page_128]

inputs of T, h and δP between 1956 and 2011. Continuous daily records of rainfall and water [SOURCE: Page_128]

flows are used. [SOURCE: Page_128]

Estimation of ETI at a daily time scale [SOURCE: Page_128]

Although the final inverted parameters are the annual values of ETI in the three pools, the model [SOURCE: Page_128]

requires the introduction of daily values of ETI. For this purpose, the evaporation dominated ETI [SOURCE: Page_128]

parameters were assigned the seasonal variability of evaporation (which is low, see Section 6). [SOURCE: Page_128]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

7.3 Model parameters inversion [SOURCE: Page_129]

ETI inversion from a Metropolis algorithm applied on lake level simulations [SOURCE: Page_129]

Eight parameters are involved in the direct water balance model (Eq. 5.) : ZR, A A0SA , A1SA , A0SN [SOURCE: Page_129]

and A1SN , ETIS , ETIN , ETIA . Let us denote m (m^1, ...m^8) the vector of the eight parameters. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Since 1237 measured lake-levels are available, the system is over constrained. Probabilistic in- [SOURCE: Page_129]

version approaches lead to a better description of parameters through a posteriori probability [SOURCE: Page_129]

density functions (PDF) instead of one single deterministic value for each parameter. Basically, [SOURCE: Page_129]

these probabilistic inverse methods consist in a Monte Carlo calculation that is a random and [SOURCE: Page_129]

oriented exploration of the parameter space and an identification of the parameter sets yielding [SOURCE: Page_129]

the best simulations through direct model-data comparisons (Eq. 3.8). The result of this guided [SOURCE: Page_129]

random walk using a priori PDF for each parameter is a collection of parameter sets that sa- [SOURCE: Page_129]

tisfactorily reproduce the observations allowing the identification of a posteriori PDF for each [SOURCE: Page_129]

parameter. Monte Carlo Markov Chain (MCMC) inversion is widely used in hydrological studies [SOURCE: Page_129]

(Dotto et al., 2011; Jeremiah et al., 2012; Kuczera and Parent, 1998; Marshall et al., 2004) and for [SOURCE: Page_129]

lake modeling (Lesht et al., 1991). [SOURCE: Page_129]

A priori probability density functions Each parameter is defined by a consistent a priori pro- [SOURCE: Page_129]

bability density function, ρ(m). Uniform laws bounded by realistic values of parameters were [SOURCE: Page_129]

considered. The soil reservoir (ZR) reflects the part of the underlying aquifer directly in connec- [SOURCE: Page_129]

tion with the lake and is bounded between 0 and 5 m depth according to realistic evaporative [SOURCE: Page_129]

rates. Upper and lower bounds of 0 and 1000, and -500 and 500 were considered for A$_0$ and [SOURCE: Page_129]

A$_1$, respectively. Because ETI are dominated by evaporation and evaporation is higher in the [SOURCE: Page_129]

northern pool, two conditions were added to satisfy the observed climatic gradient : ETIN >ETIS [SOURCE: Page_129]

and ETIA < ETIN . A value of 2000 mm yr−1 (close to the evaporation value in the southern pool) [SOURCE: Page_129]

was used for the ETI lower boundary in each pool while the upper bound is set at 3500 mm yr−1 . [SOURCE: Page_129]

Misfit function The model data comparison involves a misfit function S(m) between simulated [SOURCE: Page_129]

and measured variables. Owing to its large popularity in the hydrological community, a misfit [SOURCE: Page_129]

function derived from the Nash criterion was used to evaluate the accuracy of our hydrological [SOURCE: Page_129]

112 [SOURCE: Page_129]

### 7. Methodology

where hsi m,i are the simulated lake levels, hobs,i the existing lake level measurements (Section [SOURCE: Page_130]

6) and hmean the mean of all lake level measurements. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Metropolis Algorithm The a posteriori PDF is inferred from a guided random walk generated [SOURCE: Page_130]

by the Metropolis algorithm (Mosegaard and Tarantola, 1995), which tends to reduce the error [SOURCE: Page_130]

between model and data. The several steps of the Metropolis algorithm used here are described [SOURCE: Page_130]

in 11.3. The entire stream flow record was used to ensure a hydrologically diverse conditioning [SOURCE: Page_130]

of the model parameters/responses. [SOURCE: Page_130]

Validation of the convergence As pinpointed by Mosegaard and Tarantola (1995), defining the [SOURCE: Page_130]

acceptance rule to achieve convergence is a nontrivial task. In our study, we considered a maxi- [SOURCE: Page_130]

mum value for the misfit of 0.06 to retain a parameter set. The Nash value of 0.94 corresponds [SOURCE: Page_130]

to a systematic error of 35 cm and seems acceptable considering the cumulative uncertainties [SOURCE: Page_130]

on the lake level measurements and input variables. [SOURCE: Page_130]

FI and FE inversion from geochemical simulations [SOURCE: Page_130]

After a first inverse step to obtain ETI, a calibration of FI and FE was made. The scarcity and [SOURCE: Page_130]

spatial resolution of the chemical and isotopic data makes the inversion of parameters difficult [SOURCE: Page_130]

as compared to the hydrological modeling. A sensitivity analysis was therefore carried out for [SOURCE: Page_130]

each pool, using the ETI ranges (mean±1standard deviation) obtained from the hydrological [SOURCE: Page_130]

inversion and with values of FI and FE varying between 0 and 1. The chosen misfit function is [SOURCE: Page_130]

defined as the difference between chemical and isotopic observations (fobs,i ) and simulations [SOURCE: Page_130]

(fsi m,i ) divided by the uncertainties on the observations : [SOURCE: Page_130]

n µf                  ¶2 1X       obs,i − f si m,i S=                                                     (3.9) n i =0     σ f obs,i [SOURCE: Page_130]

## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

A value lower than 1 thus indicates that the difference between simulations and observations [SOURCE: Page_131]

is within the range of data uncertainties. This is the reason why we did not consider a unique [SOURCE: Page_131]

value of FI and FE but all the values satisfying misfit functions below 1. [SOURCE: Page_131]

Data processing to enable FE and FI calibration Simulated concentrations and isotopic com- [SOURCE: Page_131]

positions are mean values over each pool. While vertical lake water column homogeneity is ob- [SOURCE: Page_131]

served because of the shallowness of the lake, easily mixed by winds, the horizontal lake water [SOURCE: Page_131]

homogeneity is not verified in Lake Chad as described in Section 6. Therefore, only extensive [SOURCE: Page_131]

sampling campaigns can be considered as representative. From 1968 to 1971, twelve monthly [SOURCE: Page_131]

monitoring campaigns which collected between 50 and 200 samples were carried out on the [SOURCE: Page_131]

whole of Lake Chad (Carmouze, 1976). We calculated the mean sodium composition in each [SOURCE: Page_131]

pool by a volume-weighted average of all measurements for each campaign (Fig. 3.4). The un- [SOURCE: Page_131]

certainties associated with the mean values between 1968 and 1971 are related to measurement [SOURCE: Page_131]

errors and not to the horizontal variability because of the good spatial distribution. They are [SOURCE: Page_131]

thus weak and set at 0.1 mmol L−1 . From 1973 to 1977, 23 monthly monitoring campaigns were [SOURCE: Page_131]

conducted (around 15 measurements per pool per study) but covering a lower lake area (Chan- [SOURCE: Page_131]

traine, 1977, 1978; Chantraine and Lemoalle, 1976a,b). To evaluate the bias of determining a [SOURCE: Page_131]

mean composition based on a limited measurement area, we calculated the multiplying coef- [SOURCE: Page_131]

ficients between the mean concentrations of this partial area and of the entire pool using the [SOURCE: Page_131]

extensive studies by Carmouze (1976). These coefficients (between 1 and 2) were then used [SOURCE: Page_131]

to obtain estimates of the mean values on the entire domain (pools) using the corresponding [SOURCE: Page_131]

means in the restricted area for the period 1973-1977. The associated uncertainties are related [SOURCE: Page_131]

to the correcting coefficient that was calculated in a “normal” state while 1973-1977 was a drying [SOURCE: Page_131]

period. Therefore, the uncertainties of the mean values between 1973-1977 were calculated by [SOURCE: Page_131]

the difference between the corrected and uncorrected means. The data from 1973 to 1977 pro- [SOURCE: Page_131]

vide additional information on a different time period but are less constraining in the parameter [SOURCE: Page_131]

calibration process because of their high uncertainty (see Eq. 3.9). In April, July, September and [SOURCE: Page_131]

December 1969, extensive sampling campaigns and analysis of lake water δ18 O compositions [SOURCE: Page_131]

were conducted (>50 samples, Roche (1980)). We calculated the mean δ18 O in each pool by a [SOURCE: Page_131]

Calibration period Sodium data representative of mean pool values are available for the 1968- [SOURCE: Page_132]

1976 period whereas only a one-year record of isotopic data representative of mean pool va- [SOURCE: Page_132]

lues is available. Calibration was therefore only done during this time period, corresponding to [SOURCE: Page_132]

the “normal” state of Lake Chad (before 1972) and the drying transition period (1972-1976). Al- [SOURCE: Page_132]

though punctual and local, recent data enable a rough validation of the model since they give [SOURCE: Page_132]

information on the lake in a “small” state, which is very different from the calibration period. [SOURCE: Page_132]

8 Results [SOURCE: Page_132]

8.1 Estimation of the total water losses (ETI) [SOURCE: Page_132]

Convergence of the Metropolis algorithm [SOURCE: Page_132]

The convergence of the model was obtained with 25000 iterations (see 11.3). Three starting sets [SOURCE: Page_132]

of parameters were tested and we noticed that the choice of the starting point influences the [SOURCE: Page_132]

time of convergence (i.e. the number of iterations required to achieve convergence) but not the [SOURCE: Page_132]

final range of accepted parameters. This proves the robustness of the convergence. The misfit [SOURCE: Page_132]

function of the Metropolis algorithm and the 0.06 threshold is shown in 11.3, Fig. 3.9. A total [SOURCE: Page_132]

number of 6584 parameter sets were accepted, revealing the expected and well-known equifina- [SOURCE: Page_132]

lity issue in conceptual modeling, and fully justifying the use of a Bayesian approach. [SOURCE: Page_132]

Parameter distributions, uncertainty and correlation [SOURCE: Page_132]

Sets of parameters leading to a misfit below 0.06 were used to establish the posterior distribu- [SOURCE: Page_132]

tions of the eight parameters shown in Figure 3.5. The sharper the distribution is, the better [SOURCE: Page_132]

constrained the parameter is. All the parameters present unimodal distributions. They are fitted [SOURCE: Page_132]

by a normal curve except for the A0SA that is fitted by a lognormal distribution. The associated [SOURCE: Page_132]

ETI       FE       E        FI       I       E+T      T Southern       2200       0.8    1700      0.06     130     2070     370 Pool         ± 100    ± 0.2    ± 400    ± 0.1    ± 200    ± 200 Northern       2550       0.8    2040      0.11     280     2270     230 Pool         ± 100    ± 0.1    ± 300    ± 0.01   ± 25     ± 100 Archipelagos     2390      0.64    1500      0.36     850     1530      30 ± 100    ± 0.05   ± 150    ± 0.06   ± 150    ± 150 [SOURCE: Page_134]

pool (ETIS ) is poorly constrained by a normal curve and ranges between 2000 and 2400 mm [SOURCE: Page_134]

yr−1 with a mean value of 2200±117 mm yr−1. Better constrained values of 2550±80 mm.yr−1 [SOURCE: Page_134]

and 2390±100 mm.yr−1 were obtained for the northern pool and the archipelagos, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Outputs of the southern pool are dominated by flows towards the adjacent pools. The southern [SOURCE: Page_134]

pool is thus a transit reservoir while the other two pools are dead-ends, dominated by the ETI. [SOURCE: Page_134]

This is the reason why the latter two pools are more sensitive to the ETI parameter. ETI in the [SOURCE: Page_134]

southern pool is negatively correlated to ETI in the northern pool with a correlation coefficient [SOURCE: Page_134]

of -0.80 and to a lower extent to the ETI in the archipelagos (r=-0.33), which leads to higher [SOURCE: Page_134]

uncertainties of ETI. Despite these uncertainties, a robust spatial distribution of the ETI is pro- [SOURCE: Page_134]

posed. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Lake level simulations [SOURCE: Page_134]

Figure 3.6 illustrates the results of the Bayesian inverse approach for the hydrological modeling. [SOURCE: Page_134]

All the accepted parameter sets were used to calculate the corresponding lake-level time series. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Mean, maximum and minimum of the 3742 simulations are presented (Fig. 3.6) together with [SOURCE: Page_134]

available observed lake-levels in the different pools. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Simulated and observed lake levels are generally in very good agreement, which is consistent [SOURCE: Page_134]

with a Nash criterion above 0.94 obtained for all simulations. The global behavior of the lake [SOURCE: Page_134]

characterized by seasonal variability is well reproduced. Also well simulated is the disconnec- [SOURCE: Page_134]

tion that started in 1971 and the related drying of the archipelagos and of the northern pool [SOURCE: Page_134]

while the southern pool still shows a free water surface. Before 1972 and after 1988, inputs and [SOURCE: Page_134]

### 8. Results

8.2 Estimation of Infiltration, Evaporation and Transpiration [SOURCE: Page_136]

Sensitivity analysis of geochemical simulations [SOURCE: Page_136]

Based on the mean and standard deviation of ETI for each pool, a sensitivity analysis was [SOURCE: Page_136]

conducted to assess the influence of the input geochemical variables on the chemical and iso- [SOURCE: Page_136]

topic composition simulated for the three pools. The sensitivity tests based on the variations of [SOURCE: Page_136]

mean simulated annual concentrations and isotopic compositions are presented in Table 3.2. [SOURCE: Page_136]

The southern pool and the archipelagos simulated concentrations are very sensitive to varia- [SOURCE: Page_136]

tions of sodium in rivers. This effect is buffered in the northern pool. As expected, the lake is [SOURCE: Page_136]

not sensitive to rainfall which constitutes a minor input to the lake. Similarly, the isotopic com- [SOURCE: Page_136]

position of the lake is sensitive to the isotopic composition of the rivers, essentially in the sou- [SOURCE: Page_136]

thern pool that is directly fed by the main tributary (Chari-Logone). The northern pool and the [SOURCE: Page_136]

archipelagos are sensitive to the isotopic composition of the vapor and to the relative humidity. [SOURCE: Page_136]

This may be considered as a limitation of this model as these two variables and especially the [SOURCE: Page_136]

vapor isotopic composition are poorly constrained. However, the simulated evaporation slope [SOURCE: Page_136]

of 4.9 (r^2 =0.97), closely dependent on h and δA (Gonfiantini, 1986; Vallet-Coulomb et al., 2006), [SOURCE: Page_136]

is consistent with observations and thus supports the robustness of the isotopic modeling. [SOURCE: Page_136]

TABLEAU 3.2 – Sensitivity of the input geochemical variables on the geochemical simulations, using the chemical and isotopic mean annual variation during the calibration period (1968-1976). ## 3. B ILANS HYDROLOGIQUE , CHIMIQUE ET ISOTOPIQUE DU LAC T CHAD

Estimates of Evaporation (E), Infiltration (I) and Transpiration (T) [SOURCE: Page_137]

The simulated values of [Na+ ] and of the isotopic composition of water in each pool were com- [SOURCE: Page_137]

pared to the observations (Fig. 3.7). The simulations were performed for FI and FE varying bet- [SOURCE: Page_137]

ween 0 and 1, misfits are shown as functions of parameters FI and FE and parameter values [SOURCE: Page_137]

yielding misfits below 1 are retained (see Section 7). [SOURCE: Page_137]

The ranges of our best estimates for FI and FE are listed in Table 3.1. A globally acceptable agree- [SOURCE: Page_137]

ment between simulations and observations is obtained except for high concentrations of so- [SOURCE: Page_137]

dium simulated for the archipelagos and the northern pool. [SOURCE: Page_137]

In the southern pool, the acceptance zones for the FI and FE misfit functions are larger, indi- [SOURCE: Page_137]

cating again that this pool is less sensitive to E and I because its balance is dominated by the [SOURCE: Page_137]

outflows towards the adjacent pools. FI and FE are better constrained in the northern pool and [SOURCE: Page_137]

in the archipelagos. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Evaporation and infiltration rates derived from the values of FI and FE are consistent in each [SOURCE: Page_137]

pool since FI +FE is lower than 1. The difference between the two calibrated flows is interpreted [SOURCE: Page_137]

as transpiration. Evaporation is found to be higher in the northern pool than in the southern [SOURCE: Page_137]

pool and the archipelagos. Infiltration is found to be significantly higher in the archipelagos [SOURCE: Page_137]

while transpiration is significantly lower. The sum of evaporation and transpiration is higher in [SOURCE: Page_137]

the northern pool than in the southern pool, which in turn is higher than in the archipelagos. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Uncertainties on infiltration and evaporation are derived from the accepted ranges of FI and FE . [SOURCE: Page_137]

As expected, uncertainties associated with the calibrated values of evaporation and infiltration [SOURCE: Page_137]

are higher in the southern pool. In the northern pool and in the archipelagos, uncertainties are [SOURCE: Page_137]

lower but not negligible because of the scarcity and the precision of the data used to reconstruct [SOURCE: Page_137]

the mean chemical and isotopic compositions of these pools. [SOURCE: Page_137]

Geochemical simulations over the 1956-2011 period [SOURCE: Page_137]

The results of the chemical and isotopic balance calculations are presented for the three [SOURCE: Page_137]

pools on Figure 3.8 over the 1956-2011 period using the above-calibrated values of FI and FE . [SOURCE: Page_137]

### 9. Discussion

F IGURE 3.8 – Results of the chemical and isotopic modeling for each pool. The simulated sodium concen- trations and δ18 O compositions from 1956 to 2011 are represented for the calibrated range of values for FI and FE . The simulated sodium concentrations and δ18 O compositions from 1956 to 2011 are repre- sented for the calibrated range of values for FI and FE . The measured sodium concentrations and δ18 O compositions are represented by crosses for local measurements and by red dots for mean values. Co- lors refer to the corresponding monitored station (Fig. 3.4) except for purple which corresponds to local measurements away from the monitored stations. The different lake states are drawn by grey boxes and brown bands show episodes of total drying. The lower graph shows results of the simulated evolution of salt stocks : in the lake (black), in the northern pool (red), in the soil reservoir (pink), in the Southern Pool (green) and in the Archipelagos (blue). The cumulative stocks of sodium provided to the lake, essentially by rivers, and exported out of the lake by infiltration, are respectively represented by a blue dashed line and an orange dashed line. [SOURCE: Page_140]

The asymmetry of the lake is well represented in our modeling since the simulated concentra- [SOURCE: Page_140]

tions are lower in the southern pool than in the northern pool and in the archipelagos. In the [SOURCE: Page_140]

“normal” state, a simulated concentration ratio of 12 between the northern and the southern [SOURCE: Page_140]

pools is obtained while the reconstructed value based on Na data points to a ratio of 11. The [SOURCE: Page_140]

observed isotopic enrichment, δ18 O (northern pool) - δ18 O (southern pool) is 5.7 ‰ against 5.3 [SOURCE: Page_140]

‰ for simulated isotopic enrichment (Fig. 3.8). [SOURCE: Page_140]

While the most recent data cannot be fully compared to the simulations because of the bias of [SOURCE: Page_140]

comparing local and mean values, they provide some helpful information on a very different [SOURCE: Page_140]

time period as compared to that of the calibration. In fact, simulations show a relatively good [SOURCE: Page_140]

agreement with the recent data. The seasonality on isotopic compositions is slightly overesti- [SOURCE: Page_140]

mated but only a few years were monitored and it remains below the spatial variability. When [SOURCE: Page_140]

using our model to produce a simulated chronicle of salt stock in the lake through time, we ob- [SOURCE: Page_140]

serve that the total stock of salt decreases when the lake volume drops (Fig. 3.8), which is in [SOURCE: Page_140]

agreement with the observations by Carmouze (1976). [SOURCE: Page_140]

9 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_140]

9.1 Evapotranspiration rates [SOURCE: Page_140]

The calibrated value of ET in the southern pool of 2070 mm yr−1 is slightly lower than local eva- [SOURCE: Page_140]

poration measurements (2170 mm yr−1) but within the 10 % uncertainty range. The calibrated [SOURCE: Page_140]
