### 1. Préambule

1 Préambule [SOURCE: Page_190]

Dans le chapitre précédent, nous nous sommes intéressés à la variabilité du lac au cours [SOURCE: Page_190]

du dernier millénaire. Nous avons vu que les reconstitutions paléoclimatiques peinaient à [SOURCE: Page_190]

reproduire les fortes variations du niveau du lac sur cette période. Par conséquent, à l’heure [SOURCE: Page_190]

actuelle, la modélisation ne permet pas d’appréhender la dynamique du bassin versant du [SOURCE: Page_190]

Chari-Logone et a forciori la recharge des aquifères de l’échelle décennale à centennale. [SOURCE: Page_190]

Dans ce chapitre, nous utilisons divers traceurs géochimiques (36 Cl, δ18 O, δ2 H, 14 C) dans le but [SOURCE: Page_190]

de contraindre les temps de transfert dans le bassin versant ainsi que la recharge actuelle ou [SOURCE: Page_190]

récente de l’aquifère quaternaire. En particulier, la signature anthropique du 36 Cl, liée aux essais [SOURCE: Page_190]

nucléaires dans les années 60, est utilisée comme un marquage de cette période. Nous montrons [SOURCE: Page_190]

que les pluies sur le bassin versant sont dépourvus de 36 Cl des bombes alors que les eaux de [SOURCE: Page_190]

surface du bassin et certaines eaux souterraines sont marquées en 36 Cl des bombes. De cette [SOURCE: Page_190]

observation, nous pouvons contraindre le temps de transfert du Cl depuis les bassins amonts [SOURCE: Page_190]

jusque dans le lac. De plus, cet outil nous permet de localiser précisément les zones de l’aquifère [SOURCE: Page_190]

quaternaire qui enregistrent une recharge depuis les années 60. Nous estimons ainsi que 60% [SOURCE: Page_190]

de la zone de l’aquifère que nous avons étudiée est actuellement rechargée. Cette information [SOURCE: Page_190]

est essentielle puisque l’aquifère Quaternaire est la première source d’eau douce utilisée par la [SOURCE: Page_190]

population. [SOURCE: Page_190]

173 [SOURCE: Page_190]

2. Surface water transfer and groundwater recharge in the Sahelian Lake Chad Basin inferred from 36 Cl data - abstract [SOURCE: Page_192]

2 Surface water transfer and groundwater recharge in the Sahe- [SOURCE: Page_192]

lian Lake Chad Basin inferred from 36 Cl data - abstract [SOURCE: Page_192]

Authors : Camille Bouchez, Pierre Deschamps, Julio Goncalves, Bruno Hamelin, Jean-Luc Seidel, [SOURCE: Page_192]

Florence Sylvestre [SOURCE: Page_192]

Observed and projected climatic variability in the sahelian belt has brought strong interest on [SOURCE: Page_192]

the region’s groundwater resources. Sustainable management of groundwater resources requires [SOURCE: Page_192]

accurate estimates of groundwater recharge rates and a precise understanding of the hydrologi- [SOURCE: Page_192]

cal cycle at the drainage basin scale. Eighteen surface water samples and seventy groundwater [SOURCE: Page_192]

samples were collected over the Lake Chad Basin, located in the center of the Sahelian belt. The 36 Cl composition of all samples were analyzed. The bomb-produced 36 Cl measured in surface [SOURCE: Page_192]

waters points to a residence time of chlorine of about 10 years in the upper Chari-Logone ba- [SOURCE: Page_192]

sin. The 36 Cl distribution in groundwaters of the lower phreatic aquifer shows a mode around [SOURCE: Page_192]

216x^10−15 at at−1 (n=25 samples) interpreted as the natural 36 Cl/Cl background of the area. [SOURCE: Page_192]

The other groundwater samples enriched by bomb-produced 36 Cl show 36 Cl/Cl ratios between [SOURCE: Page_192]

260x^10−15 at at−1 and 5000x^10−15 at at−1 . This is used as an indicator of modern groundwaters [SOURCE: Page_192]

recharged after 1950. A present-day recharge, essentially located close to surface water bodies is [SOURCE: Page_192]

evidenced. The Chari-Logone is the major input of ground water recharge of the aquifer while [SOURCE: Page_192]

lake Chad waters are re-evaporated at 85%. Very localized high recharge rates by direct rainfall [SOURCE: Page_192]

are evidenced (165 mm yr−1 ). Pre-modern waters of the Quaternary aquifer are either waters [SOURCE: Page_192]

recharged by Lake Chad waters prior to 1950 or Holocene waters trapped in the piezometric [SOURCE: Page_192]

depression. This study proves the applicability and relevance of 36 Cl studies to constrain water [SOURCE: Page_192]

transfer and dynamics in challenging semi-arid environments. [SOURCE: Page_192]

3 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_192]

The severe and prolonged droughts observed in the Sahelian band between 1970 and 1990 [SOURCE: Page_192]

(Giorgi, 2002) as well as the uncertain rainfall projections for the next century (Cook, 2008; [SOURCE: Page_192]

175 [SOURCE: Page_192]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_193]

Druyan, 2011) have heightened attention on water resources in this region. The 3% annual [SOURCE: Page_193]

population growth in Sahel represents an additional pressure on water resources. Groundwaters [SOURCE: Page_193]

are much more resilient than surface waters and could offer large possibilities to meet water [SOURCE: Page_193]

needs (Alley et al., 2002; MacDonald et al., 2009). However, the sustainable management of [SOURCE: Page_193]

groundwaters requires evaluating the storage and the recharge rates of waters (Taylor, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_193]

Recharge is a difficult variable to assess, especially in arid environments (Scanlon et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_193]

Since most of water supply comes from phreatic aquifers, the understanding of water dynamics [SOURCE: Page_193]

and transfers of both surface and ground waters is needed. In this context, geochemical tracers [SOURCE: Page_193]

can provide valuable insights into water dynamics and recharge processes. [SOURCE: Page_193]

Tritium is an environmental tracer that is often used to date modern groundwaters since large [SOURCE: Page_193]

quantities of 3 H were produced during the thermonuclear testing. 3 H concentrations in rainfalls [SOURCE: Page_193]

reached a peak in 1963 that fingerprinted the hydrological cycle. However, due to the short [SOURCE: Page_193]

half-life of tritium (12.33 yr, Firestone et al. (1996)), this fingerprint is now attenuated because [SOURCE: Page_193]

of radioactive decay and the need for alternative tracers for groundwater has arisen. Several gas [SOURCE: Page_193]

tracers have been successfully used, such as tritiogenic 3 He, CFCs, SF$_6$ , 85 Kr. Here, we propose [SOURCE: Page_193]

to use the bomb-produced 36 Cl (Bentley et al., 1982) that shares the same environmental advan- [SOURCE: Page_193]

tages as chloride : ubiquitous, inert and conservative. Chlorine-36 is a long-lived radioisotope [SOURCE: Page_193]

with a half-life of 301000 yr. It is naturally produced in the atmosphere by cosmic-ray spallation [SOURCE: Page_193]

of 40 Ar (Huggle et al., 1996). It then mixes with marine-derived stable chlorine, falls onto the [SOURCE: Page_193]

Earth’s surface and enters the hydrological cycle. The 36 Cl deposition follows a latitudinal [SOURCE: Page_193]

distribution with greatest rates at mid-latitudes (Blinov et al., 2000; Lal and Peters, 1967). The 36 Cl/Cl ratio in precipitation depends both on 36 Cl deposition rates and Cl concentrations [SOURCE: Page_193]

in precipitation that are at first order related to the distance to the ocean (Davis et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_193]

Johnston and McDermott, 2008; Moysey et al., 2003). During the thermonuclear tests, large [SOURCE: Page_193]

quantities of 36 Cl were produced by neutron activation of 35 Cl in seawater, increasing the 36 Cl [SOURCE: Page_193]

deposition rates by as much as two or three orders of magnitude in the northern hemisphere [SOURCE: Page_193]

(Heikkilä et al., 2009). This clear anthropogenic marker, recorded in several ice-cores around [SOURCE: Page_193]

### 4. Study area

the world (Delmas et al., 2004; Synal et al., 1990), shows a peak around 1955. The attenuation [SOURCE: Page_194]

of this peak observed in natural environment allows the characterization of Cl behavior in [SOURCE: Page_194]

drainage basins, as demonstrated in the Great Lakes (Poghosyan and Sturchio, 2015). The [SOURCE: Page_194]

thermonuclear 36 Cl tracer was also used to estimate recharge rates in the unsaturated zone [SOURCE: Page_194]

(Cook et al., 1994; Liu et al., 1995; Phillips et al., 1988; Scanlon, 1992) and its application to date [SOURCE: Page_194]

modern groundwaters was demonstrated in Northeastern Germany (Tosaki et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_194]

In this study, we focus on water dynamics in the endoreic Lake Chad Basin. This large basin [SOURCE: Page_194]

of 2.5x^106 km^2 surface area, located in the center of the Sahelian belt, holds groundwaters in [SOURCE: Page_194]

its Quaternary sedimentary aquifer, which are crucial for populations. Based on 36 Cl and δ18 O [SOURCE: Page_194]

analyses of both surface water and phreatic groundwater samples, residence time of surface [SOURCE: Page_194]

water and recharge rates in the Quaternary aquifer are investigated. For the first time, 36 Cl is [SOURCE: Page_194]

used in an African drainage basin as a tracer of water transfers and groundwater recharge in [SOURCE: Page_194]

challenging semi-arid climate zone. [SOURCE: Page_194]

4 Study area [SOURCE: Page_194]

Lake Chad Basin is a 2.5 million km^2 endoreic basin, located in central Africa, extending from [SOURCE: Page_194]

7◦ E to 24◦ E and from 5◦ N to 25◦ N. The northern part of the basin is in Saharan and Sahelian cli- [SOURCE: Page_194]

matic zones, dominated by a low rainfall and a high evapotranspiration. The southern Sudanese [SOURCE: Page_194]

part of the basin is today the only hydrologically active region of the basin (about 700,000 km^2 ) [SOURCE: Page_194]

and is under the monsoon climatic regime. [SOURCE: Page_194]

The Chari-Logone River flows from the Central African Republic into the southern shore of Lake [SOURCE: Page_194]

Chad. Because of climatic and topographic South-North gradients, the Chari-Logone drainage [SOURCE: Page_194]

basin can be divided into two areas with contrasted water dynamics : the upper basin and the [SOURCE: Page_194]

lower plains (Fig. 5.1). The southern upper basin hydrology is characterized by a rapid surface [SOURCE: Page_194]

runoff during the wet season, carrying suspended matter, and a baseflow supplied by ground- [SOURCE: Page_194]

water located in alterites (Gac, 1980). The flood peak happens in October at the outlet of the [SOURCE: Page_194]

upper basin and in November at the outlet of the lower plains, revealing the one-month transfer [SOURCE: Page_194]

177 [SOURCE: Page_194]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_195]

time between the upper and lower basins. From a hydrological and chemical point of view, the [SOURCE: Page_195]

plains act as a flooding (Fig. 5.1) and deposition area. Therefore, stream flows, total dissolved [SOURCE: Page_195]

salts and suspended materials that reach Lake Chad are inherited from the upper tropical basin. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Lake Chad is fed at 85% by the Chari-Logone flows at its southern shore, while the [SOURCE: Page_195]

Komodougou-Yobe River, flowing from Nigeria and Niger to the western shore of Lake Chad, [SOURCE: Page_195]

provides a minor contribution (Olivry et al., 1996). The surface of Lake Chad decreased from [SOURCE: Page_195]

21,000 km^2 in 1960 to 2,500 km^2 in 1987 (Lemoalle et al., 2014), illustrating the consequences of [SOURCE: Page_195]

climatic changes in the Sahelian belt (Ballatore and Muhandiki, 2002; NASA Earth Observatory, [SOURCE: Page_195]

2001). This shallow lake is sub-divided into three areas, the southern pool, the northern pool [SOURCE: Page_195]

and an island area in the east named the archipelagos. Different states and morphologies [SOURCE: Page_195]

of the lake have been described as a function of lake levels (Olivry et al., 1996). When lake [SOURCE: Page_195]

levels exceed a threshold at 282.3 m, lake waters overflow in the Bahr-el-Ghazal channel which [SOURCE: Page_195]

### 4. Study area

drains water towards the Bodele depression (Fig. 5.1). Lake Chad is a concentrating pool where [SOURCE: Page_196]

tropical-originating waters are submitted to high evaporation rates of about 2,000 mm yr−1 with [SOURCE: Page_196]

a 1 to 2 yr residence time of waters (Roche, 1980). [SOURCE: Page_196]

The lake and the lower Chari-Logone River are connected to an unconfined aquifer of 500,000 [SOURCE: Page_196]

km^2 in the central part of the LCB (Fig. 5.1). This aquifer is composed by silts, sands and clays, [SOURCE: Page_196]

deposited during alternating highstand and lowstand of the lake during the Quaternary. The thi- [SOURCE: Page_196]

ckness of the aquifer ranges from 0 to 180 m with an average of around 100 m. The scatter in [SOURCE: Page_196]

the chemical signature of groundwaters demonstrates that it is strongly heterogeneous (Abdera- [SOURCE: Page_196]

mane et al., 2013; Chouret, 1977; Gaultier, 2004; Zairi, 2008) and suggests that it may be multi- [SOURCE: Page_196]

layered (Ngatcha et al., 2007). This aquifer is separated from the underlying artesian aquifer of [SOURCE: Page_196]

the Continental Terminal and Pliocene by a thick clay layer (Schneider, 2004). [SOURCE: Page_196]

The water table of the aquifer is characterized by three major piezometric depressions around [SOURCE: Page_196]

the lake (Chari Baguirmi, Kadzell, north of the lake) and two piezometric domes (Harr, Kanem) [SOURCE: Page_196]

shown in Fig. 5.1. Similar features have already been described in other phreatic aquifers in the [SOURCE: Page_196]

Sahelian belt (Aranyossy and Ndiaye, 1993; Archambault, 1960). Although they are well charac- [SOURCE: Page_196]

terised, the explanation for such depressions is still debated. The main assumption is a high [SOURCE: Page_196]

and localized evapotranspiration rate associated with low hydraulic conductivities (Aranyossy [SOURCE: Page_196]

and Ndiaye, 1993). The plausibility of this assumption was confirmed in the LCB by the steady [SOURCE: Page_196]

state regional flow model developed by Boronina and Ramillien (2008). In present-day condi- [SOURCE: Page_196]

tions, the aquifer is recharged by diffuse rainfall with a median recharge estimated in Northern [SOURCE: Page_196]

Nigeria around 43 mm yr−1 (Edmunds et al., 2002) against 3.5 mm yr−1 estimated in Niger (Le- [SOURCE: Page_196]

duc et al., 2000). Isotopic studies suggest that the main recharge occurred in past humid periods [SOURCE: Page_196]

before 4,000 years BP (Leblanc, 2002; Leduc et al., 2000). This can be related to the last megalake [SOURCE: Page_196]

phase of Lake Chad, estimated at 340,000 km^2 by spatial studies (Leblanc et al., 2006; Schuster [SOURCE: Page_196]

et al., 2005), which occurred during the African Humid Period (Amaral et al., 2013; Servant and [SOURCE: Page_196]

Servant-Vildary, 1980). [SOURCE: Page_196]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_197]

5 Samples and method [SOURCE: Page_197]

5.1 Sampling [SOURCE: Page_197]

Eighteen surface water samples, 5 rainfall samples and 70 Quaternary aquifer groundwater [SOURCE: Page_197]

samples were collected from 2008 to 2013 in the southern part of the Lake Chad Basin. Sur- [SOURCE: Page_197]

face water have been sampled in the upper Logone River, the downstream Chari-Logone River, [SOURCE: Page_197]

the southern pool of Lake Chad and the archipelagos (Fig. 5.1). The Quaternary aquifer has been [SOURCE: Page_197]

sampled extensively in its southern chadian part between 2011 and 2013. We collected ground- [SOURCE: Page_197]

waters from wells located near the Chari-Logone River, around Lake Chad, in the center of the [SOURCE: Page_197]

piezometric depressions and along the Bahr El Ghazal axis (Fig. 5.1). [SOURCE: Page_197]

On site analysis included temperature, electrical conductivity, dissolved oxygen concentration [SOURCE: Page_197]

and pH. Groundwater samples were collected from existing boreholes preliminarily flushed out. [SOURCE: Page_197]

Water samples were collected in pre-cleaned HDPE bottles for major ions and 36 Cl analyses and [SOURCE: Page_197]

in pre-cleaned amber glass bottles for stable isotopes and 14 C analyses. All samples were stored [SOURCE: Page_197]

at 4◦ C. [SOURCE: Page_197]

5.2 Chemical preparation and measurement [SOURCE: Page_197]

Major ion (Na+ , K+ , Ca$_2$+ , Mg$_2$+ , Cl− , SO$_2$−    − 4 , NO$_3$ ) analyses were carried out at HydroSciences [SOURCE: Page_197]

Montpellier on filtered aliquots by ion chromatography. The concentration of bicarbonate ion [SOURCE: Page_197]

was determined by titration on site. Measurements of 36 Cl were carried out by Accelerator Mass [SOURCE: Page_197]

Spectrometry (AMS) (Elmore et al., 1979) at the French AMS National facility, ASTER at CE- [SOURCE: Page_197]

REGE, Aix en Provence. Because of low chlorine content samples, the addition of 35 Cl enriched [SOURCE: Page_197]

spike was required to reach a chlorine total amount of 2 mg and to precisely determine the Cl [SOURCE: Page_197]

concentration using ID-AMS technique (Bouchez et al., 2015b). Water samples were processed [SOURCE: Page_197]

in batches of 10-15 samples with one spiked blank and one unspiked blank at the end of each [SOURCE: Page_197]

batch to estimate chemical contaminations on the chlorine concentration and 36 Cl/Cl respecti- [SOURCE: Page_197]

vely. Ultra-pure reagents were used to minimize contamination with natural chloride. Chlorine [SOURCE: Page_197]

was extracted by precipitation of AgCl, which was then re-dissolved in aqueous NH$_3$ . A saturated [SOURCE: Page_197]

in AMS measurements. Purified AgCl precipitates were obtained after re-precipitation in nitric [SOURCE: Page_198]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_199]

The first group consists of low concentrated, HCO$_3$ -Ca type water samples, chemically similar to [SOURCE: Page_199]

surface waters (Table. 5.1). The second group is composed of Na-enriched, moderately concen- [SOURCE: Page_199]

trated samples (Table. 5.1). The third group comprises SO$_4$ -Cl-Na highly concentrated waters [SOURCE: Page_199]

(Table. 5.1). Samples of the fourth group contain high concentrations of nitrate and chloride, [SOURCE: Page_199]

representative of anthropogenic pollution (Table. 5.1). The chemical facies and conductivities [SOURCE: Page_199]

of all samples are shown on a Piper diagram (Fig. 5.2). We observe a global evolution trend of [SOURCE: Page_199]

ground water chemistry from HCO$_3$ -Ca type to Cl-SO$_4$ -Na type with a significant increase of the [SOURCE: Page_199]

conductivity. This is likely to be related to an increase of water residence time with evapora- [SOURCE: Page_199]

tion inducing water-rock interaction and Ca/Na exchanges. The important increase in sulphate [SOURCE: Page_199]

concentration cannot be solely explained by evaporation and probably implies the dissolution [SOURCE: Page_199]

of brine evaporites. [SOURCE: Page_199]

### 6. Results

phic location : groundwaters of groups 1 and 2 are mainly located near the Chari-Logone River [SOURCE: Page_200]

and near Lake Chad while groundwaters of groups 3 and 4 correspond of groundwater samples [SOURCE: Page_200]

of the Bahr-El-Ghazal channel and the piezometric depressions. [SOURCE: Page_200]

36 6.2          Cl/Cl signature of surface waters [SOURCE: Page_200]

The 36 Cl/Cl signature is considered rather than the 36 Cl contents since this ratio is unaffected [SOURCE: Page_200]

by evaporative enrichment, which is dominant in semi-arid environments. 36 Cl/Cl ratios were [SOURCE: Page_200]

analyzed for 18 surface waters samples and 5 rainfall samples (Table 5.3). [SOURCE: Page_200]

The uncertainties associated with rainfall data are very high because of very low chlorine [SOURCE: Page_200]

contents. Rainfalls were sampled in July 2014 at NDjamena (Chad) and in September 2013 at [SOURCE: Page_200]

NGaoundéré (North Cameroon). Provided the annual variability of the 36 Cl/Cl distribution in [SOURCE: Page_200]

rainfall, these discrete monthly rainfall measurements are not fully representative. However, [SOURCE: Page_200]

they show good consistency with each other and bring constraints for a low 36 Cl/Cl signature [SOURCE: Page_200]

(below 300x^10−15 at at−1 ) for rainfalls in the drainage basin. The 36 Cl/Cl ratios of surface waters [SOURCE: Page_200]

are higher, ranging from 600x^10−15 up to 1450x^10−15 at at−1 . The Chari-Logone 36 Cl/Cl is stable [SOURCE: Page_200]

during the two investigated years with respect to the uncertainties (Table 5.3) The weighted ave- [SOURCE: Page_200]

rage is (1157±43)x^10−15 at at−1 (n=10). Lake Chad waters in the Southern Pool directly fed by the [SOURCE: Page_200]

Chari-Logone have similar 36 Cl/Cl signature (1106±60)x^10−15 at at−1 (n=4). The Komadougou [SOURCE: Page_200]

Yobe River has a lower 36 Cl/Cl signature (669±77)x^10−15 at at−1 (n=1) than the Chari-Logone [SOURCE: Page_200]

but higher than rainfalls. Lake Chad waters in the eastern archipelagos have a lower 36 Cl/Cl [SOURCE: Page_200]

(617±55)x^10−15 at at−1 (n=2) signature than in the southern pool. [SOURCE: Page_200]

36 6.3          Cl/Cl signature of groundwaters of the Quaternary aquifer [SOURCE: Page_200]

Seventy groundwater samples were analyzed for 36 Cl/Cl (Table 5.3). The 36 Cl signature of [SOURCE: Page_200]

the Quaternary aquifer shows very large dispersion with a range going from 100x^10−15 up to [SOURCE: Page_200]

5100x^10−15 at at−1 . The results are presented in a 36 Cl/Cl as a function of Cl diagram (Fig. 5.3). [SOURCE: Page_200]

We observe a broad consistency between water chemistry, highlighted by the different groups, [SOURCE: Page_200]

and the 36 Cl/Cl signature. Two modes can be identified in the 36 Cl/Cl distribution : water [SOURCE: Page_200]

TABLEAU 5.3 – Water sample characteristics (suite). GW : Groundwater ; SW : Suface Water ; Lake Chad SP : Southern Pool ; Lake Chad A : Archipelagos. [SOURCE: Page_202]

samples with 36 Cl/Cl of about 200x^10−15 at at−1 (groundwaters of groups 2 and 3) and water [SOURCE: Page_202]

samples with higher 36 Cl/Cl (groundwaters of groups 1 and 2 and surface waters). Two samples [SOURCE: Page_202]

of the fourth group fall out of this distribution with 36 Cl/Cl below 150x^10−15 at at−1 . [SOURCE: Page_202]

6.4     14 C , δ13 C, δ18 O and δ2 H data [SOURCE: Page_202]

The isotopic compositions of the Dissolved Inorganic Carbon (δ13 C) and the stable isotopic [SOURCE: Page_202]

composition of water (δ18 O, δ2 H) were analyzed for 13 and 18 groundwater samples respecti- [SOURCE: Page_202]

vely. Results show 14 C ratio all over 50 pmc, going from 54 to 92 pmc, indicating modern ground [SOURCE: Page_202]

waters. Except for two samples with a δ13 C lower than -20‰, δ13 C range between -10‰ and [SOURCE: Page_202]

-4‰, indicating different sources of the DIC. The δ18 O of these groundwaters range between [SOURCE: Page_202]

-4.8‰ and +6.1‰ and plot along a -7.3+5.7xδ18 O (r^2 =0.98) evaporation line. We clearly distin- [SOURCE: Page_202]

guish depleted waters with δ18 O between -5‰ and -2‰ and enriched water with δ18 O between [SOURCE: Page_202]

2‰ and 6‰. Samples with the highest 14 C contents (around 90 pmc) are enriched and show [SOURCE: Page_202]

δ13 C over -10‰. [SOURCE: Page_202]

6.5 Geographic distribution [SOURCE: Page_203]

The 36 Cl and 18 O isotopic composition of ground water samples are shown on a map of the Qua- [SOURCE: Page_203]

ternary aquifer (Fig. 5.4). The high 36 Cl/Cl ratios are located close to surface waters (both rivers [SOURCE: Page_203]

and Lake Chad) and below flood plains. Low 36 Cl/Cl ratios are found in the waters localized in [SOURCE: Page_203]

the north of the lake, in the piezometric depression and in the Bahr El Ghazal channel. Enriched [SOURCE: Page_203]

waters are localized around Lake Chad and in the Bahr El Ghazal channel. [SOURCE: Page_203]

7 Interpretation and discussion [SOURCE: Page_204]

36 7.1             Cl/Cl natural background ratio recorded in the unconfined aquifer [SOURCE: Page_204]

36 Cl/Cl ratios in groundwaters of the Quaternary aquifer show a distribution dominated by a [SOURCE: Page_204]

200x^10−15 at at−1 value. Using a chi-2 test, we identified 25 samples with consistent values ac- [SOURCE: Page_204]

cording to their uncertainties. We interpret the mean value of (216±2)x^10−15 at at−1 as the na- [SOURCE: Page_204]

tural background 36 Cl/Cl ratio of the Basin. The present-day rainfall ratios, even if associated [SOURCE: Page_204]

with high uncertainties, are consistent with this natural background value. According to An- [SOURCE: Page_204]

drews et al. (1994), the natural Cl fallout can thus be calculated using the following equation : [SOURCE: Page_204]

R × [Cl − ]P × P × 10−3 × NA F=                                                                                                (5.1) 3.15 × 107 × 35.5 [SOURCE: Page_204]

where R is the measured 36 Cl/Cl ratio, P the mean annual precipitation (mm yr−1 ), [Cl− ]P the [SOURCE: Page_204]

chlorine concentration in precipitation (in mg L−1 ) and Na the Avogadro number. [SOURCE: Page_204]

We used a mean value of 800±300 mm yr−1 for the mean annual precipitation value, the Cl− [SOURCE: Page_204]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_205]

concentration is 0.1±0.2 mg L−1 (the average of measured [Cl− ]P ) and the 36 Cl/Cl ratio is the [SOURCE: Page_205]

natural background (i.e. (216±2)x^10−15 at at−1 ). The estimated fallout rate of 9±5 at m−2 s−1 [SOURCE: Page_205]

is consistent with the natural 36 Cl fallout modeling of 9 at m−2 s−1 between 10°N and 20°N lati- [SOURCE: Page_205]

tudes (Blinov et al., 2000). The relatively high 216x^10−15 at at−1 value, despite the low fallout rate, [SOURCE: Page_205]

is related to the very low chlorine content in rainfall that is a consequence of the long distance [SOURCE: Page_205]

to the ocean (1500 km). [SOURCE: Page_205]

This background value is of major importance as i) it is the first estimation of the pre-bomb 36 Cl fallout at this latitude, ii) it supports latitudinal models and iii) it gives robust information [SOURCE: Page_205]

on the initial 36 Cl/Cl ratio in the area, which is a key issue for water age determination using 36 Cl radioactive decay (Davis et al., 1998). In Egypt, the initial ratio of 131x^10−15 at at−1 estima- [SOURCE: Page_205]

ted from groundwater measurements is lower, consistently with shorter distance to the ocean [SOURCE: Page_205]

(Patterson et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_205]

7.2 The bomb imprint in the surface waters of the LCB [SOURCE: Page_205]

The Chari-Logone River and the southern pool of Lake Chad, with an average ratio of [SOURCE: Page_205]

(1125±50)x^10−15 at at−1 , show a 36 Cl signature much higher than the estimated natural [SOURCE: Page_205]

background of 216x^10−15 . The 36 Cl enrichment in surface waters can be related to the large [SOURCE: Page_205]

bomb-produced 36 Cl, which implies a delayed delivery to the lake after long-term storage of Cl [SOURCE: Page_205]

within the drainage basin. The chemical signature of the Chari-Logone River is inherited from [SOURCE: Page_205]

the upper drainage basin (Gac, 1980), which is supported by our measurements upstream (500 [SOURCE: Page_205]

km away from the lake, Fig. 5.1) and downstream Chari-Logone having similar 36 Cl/Cl ratios. [SOURCE: Page_205]

While the recycling of bomb-produced 36 Cl from the vegetation back to the atmosphere was [SOURCE: Page_205]

previously evidenced (Scheffel et al., 1999), our study shows that rainfall both in the upper part [SOURCE: Page_205]

(Ngaoundere) and in the lower plains (Ndjamena) have 36 Cl ratios close to the natural ratio. [SOURCE: Page_205]

Therefore, the 36 Cl enrichment, measured in surface waters in upper and lower LCB, points to [SOURCE: Page_205]

a stored thermonuclear 36 Cl within the upper drainage basin. [SOURCE: Page_205]

The river flows in the upper basin are driven by rapid runoff and slow baseflow with 38% of the [SOURCE: Page_205]

F IGURE 5.5 – Decomposition of runoff vs baseflow in the hydrogram of the Chari-Logone upper basin (Gac, 1980). The surface runoff is correlated to high suspended matter and lower total dissolved salts. [Na+ ] measured in the river are shown by plain green circles while reconstructed [Na+ ] from the ru- noff/baseflow decomposition are shown by green rectangles. Good correlation between the measured and reconstructed concentrations confirms the hydrogram decomposition. It leads to an annual propor- tion of 38% of dissolved Na+ transported rapidly with runoff against 62% slowly transported with base- flow. [SOURCE: Page_206]

In order to evaluate the residence time of chlorine in the upper basin, a mixing model of the 36 Cl/Cl of the river (RI) at time t is built between a fast-moving Cl with runoff (RF) and a slowly- [SOURCE: Page_206]

moving Cl with baseflow (BF), such as : [SOURCE: Page_206]

36    ¯           36 ¯¯             36 ¯¯ Cl ¯¯             Cl ¯              Cl ¯ = 0.38 ×          + 0.62 ×                                            (5.2) Cl RI,t ¯            Cl RF,t ¯             Cl ¯BF,t [SOURCE: Page_206]

Because of the high variability of 36 Cl fallout since the early 1950’s, the mixing model is used [SOURCE: Page_206]

in transient state at yearly time step between 1940 and 2015. Although we did not measure [SOURCE: Page_206]

the 36 Cl/Cl ratio in the Chari-Logone during a complete hydrological cycle, we assume that the [SOURCE: Page_206]

mean ratio of 1157x^10−15 at at−1 is representative of the sampling years (2011, 2012, 2013). This [SOURCE: Page_206]

assumption is supported by the low 36 Cl/Cl variability of the Chari-Logone waters despite sam- [SOURCE: Page_206]

pling months both at low (june) and high (november) river heads sampling months (Table 5.3). [SOURCE: Page_206]

Assuming that chlorine deposition has not changed during this time period, the 36 Cl/Cl va- [SOURCE: Page_206]

riability is only due to the subsequent 36 Cl anthropogenic input. The 36 Cl/ClRF ratio in runoff [SOURCE: Page_206]

is assumed equal to the rainfall ratio of the same year, as no recycling in the atmosphere is [SOURCE: Page_206]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_207]

evidenced. [SOURCE: Page_207]

The 36 Cl/ClRF in baseflow is estimated from a lumped parameter model using an exponential [SOURCE: Page_207]

weighting function, suitable to represent mixing in an unconfined aquifer (Turnadge and Smer- [SOURCE: Page_207]

don, 2014). On a 50 years time period, radioactive decay of 36 Cl can be neglected. This model [SOURCE: Page_207]

involves one parameter associated to residence time, following the equation (TRACER, Bayari [SOURCE: Page_207]

(2002)) : [SOURCE: Page_207]

36          Z t 36 ¯ t − t^0 µ         ¶ Cl              Cl ¯¯   0   1 (t ) =             (t ) × × exp −          dt 0                    (5.3) Cl          −∞ Cl i n ¯       τ          τ [SOURCE: Page_207]

where (36 Cl /Cl)i n is the bomb-produced 36 Cl ratio time series input and τ (yr−1 ) is the lumped [SOURCE: Page_207]

model parameter. Records of the bomb-produced 36 Cl fallout from the Dye-3 ice core in Green- [SOURCE: Page_207]

land (Synal et al., 1990) have been widely used as input scenario at high latitude (Poghosyan [SOURCE: Page_207]

and Sturchio, 2015; Tosaki et al., 2011). However, the natural dynamics of 36 Cl fallout at low [SOURCE: Page_207]

latitude is very different from high latitude deposits (Blinov et al., 2000; Lal and Peters, 1967). [SOURCE: Page_207]

The use of the anthropogenic 36 Cl fallout recorded in Greenland is likely not suitable for the [SOURCE: Page_207]

LCB. That is the reason why we also used bomb-produced 36 Cl fallout simulations (Heikkilä [SOURCE: Page_207]

et al., 2009), averaged on the available map grid points corresponding to the LCB (n=7). The [SOURCE: Page_207]

latter 36 Cl time series leads to a total anthropogenic deposition of 9x^1012 at m−2 against 2x^1012 [SOURCE: Page_207]

at m−2 at Dye-3. The 36 Cl fallout time series are converted into 36 Cl/Cl time series, using Eq. [SOURCE: Page_207]

(1) with constant mean annual rainfall of the upper basin of 1200 mm yr−1 (Gac, 1980) and a [SOURCE: Page_207]

rainfall chlorine concentration of 0.1 mg L−1 . [SOURCE: Page_207]

The transient mixing model results are shown Figure 5.6. An agreement between simulated and [SOURCE: Page_207]

measured 36 Cl/Cl in rivers is obtained for chlorine residence time in the baseflow of about 10 [SOURCE: Page_207]

years. The 36 Cl fallout time series is of low impact on the calibrated residence time. The 10-years [SOURCE: Page_207]

residence time value seems consistent with a conservative water transfer in alterite groundwa- [SOURCE: Page_207]

ters. According to the size of the basin, it is very difficult to estimate the mean residence time of [SOURCE: Page_207]

alterite groundwaters. Our method integrate the geochemical signal of all the basin and allows [SOURCE: Page_207]

### 7. Interpretation and discussion

F IGURE 5.6 – Residence time of chlorine in the Chari-Logone drainage basin. Two atmospheric 36 Cl/Cl time series are used (dotted lines) derived from : the fallout ice-record of Dye-3 (Synal et al., 1990) in purple and the fallout simulation of the total bomb-produced 36 Cl by Heikkilä et al. (2009), suitable for Lake Chad in green. 36 Cl/Cl in baseflow (thin lines) are calculated with an exponential lumped model parameter. 36 Cl/Cl in rivers (thick lines) are calculated by the mixing between runoff and baseflow (Eq. (5.2), Fig. 5.5). The displayed residence time allows to reproduce the 36 Cl/Cl measured in surface waters (blue dot). [SOURCE: Page_208]

However the simulated decrease of 36 Cl/Cl ratios of about 200x^10−15 at at−1 between 2011 and [SOURCE: Page_208]

2014 is in contradiction with the constancy of the measured ratios. This could be explained by a [SOURCE: Page_208]

small part of non-conservative Cl as previously highlighted in forested systems containing low [SOURCE: Page_208]

Cl− concentrations (Milton et al., 2003) or in the Great Lake Basin (Poghosyan and Sturchio, [SOURCE: Page_208]

2015). Indeed, the uptake of chlorine as methyl chloride either by the organic matter of the [SOURCE: Page_208]

vegetation or by microorganisms in soils can induce a slow leaching of the bomb pulse into [SOURCE: Page_208]

surface waters (Blinov et al., 2000; Cornett et al., 1997; Davis et al., 2001; Milton et al., 2003; [SOURCE: Page_208]

Münsterer et al., 2012). The proportion of Cl long-term uptake was estimated at about 4% of [SOURCE: Page_208]

the total chhlorine deposition (Bastviken et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_208]

191 [SOURCE: Page_208]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_209]

The observed discrepancies between 36 Cl/Cl ratios in the Chari-Logone and the Komadougou [SOURCE: Page_209]

Yobe could be attributed to different groundwater dynamics in their upper drainage basin. The [SOURCE: Page_209]

lower signature of the archipelagos (eastern part of the lake) compared to the southern pool [SOURCE: Page_209]

indicates another input of chlorine free from bomb-produced 36 Cl that would need further in- [SOURCE: Page_209]

vestigation. [SOURCE: Page_209]

7.3 Bomb-bearing groundwaters : 36 Cl a suitable tracer of modern recharge [SOURCE: Page_209]

Two samples have 36 Cl/Cl significantly lower than 216x^10−15 at at−1 (Fig. 5.3) likely due to dis- [SOURCE: Page_209]

solution of evaporites that enriched those two samples in chlorine. No dissolution of evaporites [SOURCE: Page_209]

is evidenced for all other samples. This is consistent with field observations that do not report [SOURCE: Page_209]

halite deposition in the Lake Chad Basin. [SOURCE: Page_209]

Apart from the constant value of 216x^10−15 at at−1 observed in the Quaternary aquifer, high [SOURCE: Page_209]

ratios are observed ranging from 260x^10−15 at at−1 up to 5000x^10−15 at at−1 . This indicates en- [SOURCE: Page_209]

richment in 36 Cl related to nuclear tests. Therefore the 36 Cl bomb record in the groundwaters [SOURCE: Page_209]

is a proxy of modern waters recharged after 1960, while groundwaters with a 36 Cl signature [SOURCE: Page_209]

around 200x^10−15 at at−1 indicate a recharge prior to 1960. In spite of the highly heterogeneous [SOURCE: Page_209]

chemical distribution of the Quaternary aquifer groundwaters (Abderamane et al., 2013; Chou- [SOURCE: Page_209]

ret, 1977; Gaultier, 2004; Zairi, 2008), a clear correlation appears between the pre-modern 36 Cl [SOURCE: Page_209]

contents and the highest conductivities and evolved chemical facies (Fig. 5.3), consistent with [SOURCE: Page_209]

longer groundwater residence time for these samples. These groundwaters are located in the [SOURCE: Page_209]

piezometric depressions and in the Bahr El Ghazal channel. [SOURCE: Page_209]

7.4 Modern Quaternary aquifer recharge origin and rates [SOURCE: Page_209]

Modern recharge is clearly evidenced, by the thermonuclear 36 Cl in 60% of our total sampling [SOURCE: Page_209]

distribution. Modern recharge occurs in water samples that have a chemical composition close [SOURCE: Page_209]

to surface waters (Groups 1 and 2). However, based on the confrontation of 36 Cl and δ18 O signa- [SOURCE: Page_209]

tures, we can disentangle between different origins of the groundwater recharge (Fig. 5.7). [SOURCE: Page_209]

F IGURE 5.7 – Interpretation of the chlorine and hydrological dynamics in the Lake Chad Basin. [SOURCE: Page_210]

Modern lake-like groundwaters [SOURCE: Page_210]

Around Lake Chad, groundwaters have a chemical and isotopic composition close to the lake [SOURCE: Page_210]

waters (high 36 Cl/Cl, 18 O-enriched waters, chemical groups 1 and 2, Fig. 5.7). They can be in- [SOURCE: Page_210]

terpreted as modern infiltration of lake waters into the Quaternary aquifer. Previous tritium [SOURCE: Page_210]

concentrations measured on the north-eastern shore of Lake Chad also support modern re- [SOURCE: Page_210]

193 [SOURCE: Page_210]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_211]

charge in this area (Vassolo, 2010). The evaporation of lake waters after their infiltration into [SOURCE: Page_211]

the Quaternary aquifer can be estimated based on a chlorine mass balance applied on samples [SOURCE: Page_211]

isotopically identified as lake-like waters. Using a mean value of 1 mg L−1 [Cl− ] in the archipe- [SOURCE: Page_211]

lagos, it leads to a mean re-evaporation of lake-waters of 85% in the Quaternary aquifer. This [SOURCE: Page_211]

result highly supports previous estimates of a high evaporation of infiltrated lake-waters into [SOURCE: Page_211]

the Quaternary aquifer (Chap. 3). [SOURCE: Page_211]

Modern recharge through the Chari-Logone system In the south, contrary to the lake-like [SOURCE: Page_211]

groudwaters, these samples have a depleted 18 O composition (Fig. 5.7). These waters are as- [SOURCE: Page_211]

sociated to the Chari-Logone system. Most samples have 36 Cl/Cl ratios above 1200x^10−15 at [SOURCE: Page_211]

at−1 , which indicate a recharge during the last fifty years. The moderate values of 36 Cl/Cl can [SOURCE: Page_211]

be interpreted as a mixing between the modern Chari-Logone end member and a pre-modern [SOURCE: Page_211]

end-member. We built a mixing line between the two end-members (Fig. 5.8). The Chari-Logone [SOURCE: Page_211]

end-member has a chlorine concentration of 0.1 mg L−1 and the 36 Cl/Cl ratio of this end mem- [SOURCE: Page_211]

ber is taken as the highest measurement, i.e. 5000x^10−15 at at−1 . The pre-modern end member 36 Cl/Cl ratio (216x^10−15 at at−1 ) is well constrained and we used the chlorine concentration of [SOURCE: Page_211]

the sample with lowest 36 Cl/Cl (i.e. 5 mg L−1 ). The increase of chlorine concentration with res- [SOURCE: Page_211]

pect to the mixing line is interpreted as evaporation which allows to calculate a relatively low [SOURCE: Page_211]

evaporation with a mean value at 50%. [SOURCE: Page_211]

Rainfall recharged samples Four samples cannot be explained by this mixing model as they [SOURCE: Page_211]

plot below the mixing line. Their low chloride concentrations and low 36 Cl/Cl, very close to the [SOURCE: Page_211]

present-day rainfall composition, suggest recent infiltrated rainfalls. Based on a chlorine mass [SOURCE: Page_211]

balance (Love et al., 2000), we can calculate recharge rates of groundwater samples recharged [SOURCE: Page_211]

only by rainfall, using mean precipitation of 400 mm yr−1 and chloride concentration of 0.1 mg [SOURCE: Page_211]

L−1 . It leads to very high recharge rates with a mean value of 165 mm yr−1 . This value is much [SOURCE: Page_211]

higher than previous diffuse recharge values estimated in Niger and Nigeria (Edmunds et al., [SOURCE: Page_211]

2002; Leduc et al., 2000). However, it corresponds to focused recharge rates, localized on sand [SOURCE: Page_211]

dunes, with high permeability. Diffuse recharge seems negligible in the North of the Basin. [SOURCE: Page_211]

F IGURE 5.8 – Mixing line model between the Chari-Logone end-member and the pre-modern end- member to explain the variability of 36 Cl/Cl ratios measured in the Quaternary Aquifer. The enrichment in chlorine is interpreted by evaporative process. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Pre-modern Quaternary aquifer recharge Pre-modern samples are located at the outlets [SOURCE: Page_212]

of groundwater flowpaths. Based on the water stable isotopic composition, we can clearly [SOURCE: Page_212]

distinguish 18 O-depleted and 18 O-enriched waters. The Bahr El Ghazal waters are very enriched [SOURCE: Page_212]

waters close to Lake Chad stable signature. The Bahr El Ghazal is the natural overflow channel [SOURCE: Page_212]

of Lake Chad waters when its level exceeds 283 m. The last occurrence of large water lake [SOURCE: Page_212]

outflow into the Bahr el Ghazal was reported during the 17t h century (Maley, 2010). Therefore [SOURCE: Page_212]

the isotopic signatures of Bahr El Ghazal waters indicate lake water infiltrated during Lake [SOURCE: Page_212]

Chad highstand before the anthropogenic release of 36 Cl in the atmosphere. Similarly to the [SOURCE: Page_212]

estimation of the present-day evaporation of Lake Chad waters in the aquifer, an evaporation [SOURCE: Page_212]

of 95% is estimated. [SOURCE: Page_212]

Pre-modern waters that are not in the axis of the Bahr El Ghazal channel are localized in the [SOURCE: Page_212]

piezometric depressions and characterized by 18 O-depleted waters. This is in agreement with [SOURCE: Page_212]

previous measurements in water samples of the center of the depressions of δ18 O around -4‰, [SOURCE: Page_212]

δ13 C around -10‰ and 14 C as low as 7 pmc (Djoret, 2000). Therefore these pre-modern ground- [SOURCE: Page_212]

waters are probably much older than the pre-modern Bahr El Ghazal groundwaters. They were [SOURCE: Page_212]

195 [SOURCE: Page_212]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_213]

related to the mid-Holocene African Humid Period (Djoret, 2000), which corresponds to a large [SOURCE: Page_213]

extension of lakes in North Africa (Gasse, 2000) and a filling of phreatic aquifers (Lézine et al., [SOURCE: Page_213]

2011). [SOURCE: Page_213]

8 Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_213]

The Lake Chad Basin hydrology is described using a multiple tracer investigation and the 36 Cl [SOURCE: Page_213]

is shown to be a powerful tool to constrain surface and groundwater dynamics in the basin. [SOURCE: Page_213]

The surface water 36 Cl signature allows estimating a residence time of chlorine in the upper [SOURCE: Page_213]

basin of about 10 years. This value is of first importance as 1) many studies are based on the [SOURCE: Page_213]

conservative behavior of chlorine to estimate hydrological dynamics in drainage basin 2) it gives [SOURCE: Page_213]

an integrated value of water residence time in a large basin, which is often hard to assess. [SOURCE: Page_213]

The groundwater distribution of 36 Cl in the Quaternary aquifer shows two trends that are ap- [SOURCE: Page_213]

proximately in agreement with the determination of chemical facies-based groups. The first [SOURCE: Page_213]

mode of values comprises 25 samples with a mean value at 216x^10−15 at at−1 . It corresponds [SOURCE: Page_213]

to the natural background of 36 Cl deposition of 7 at m−2 s−1 and is consistent with latitudinal [SOURCE: Page_213]

fallout models. Values up to 5000x^10−15 at at−1 were interpreted as groundwaters that imprin- [SOURCE: Page_213]

ted the nuclear tests dating back to the 1950s. This bomb-produced 36 Cl fingerprints allows to [SOURCE: Page_213]

localize the present-day recharge close to surface waters, which is also supported by oxygen-18 [SOURCE: Page_213]

signature. The modern recharge of the Quaternary aquifer is essentially related to the Chari- [SOURCE: Page_213]

Logone system while the contribution of Lake Chad is weak. Two different old groundwater end [SOURCE: Page_213]

members were identified : Lake Chad waters prior to 1950 and Holocene waters trapped in the [SOURCE: Page_213]

piezometric depression. This study proves the ability of 36 Cl as a hydrological tracer and should [SOURCE: Page_213]

enlarge the use of 36 Cl to estimate surface and ground water transfer in semi-arid environments. [SOURCE: Page_213]

196 [SOURCE: Page_213]

### 9. Epilogue : une drainance ascendante de l’aquifère profond vers l’aquifère quaternaire ?

9 Epilogue : une drainance ascendante de l’aquifère profond vers [SOURCE: Page_214]

l’aquifère quaternaire ? [SOURCE: Page_214]

Nous avons mis en évidence des eaux anciennes dans la dépression du Chari-Baguirmi et, bien [SOURCE: Page_214]

que cela soit sous-contraint, ces eaux sont interprétées comme des reliques de la Période Hu- [SOURCE: Page_214]

mide Africaine. Cette dépression s’étend sur une surface de près de 10 000 km^2 et la profondeur [SOURCE: Page_214]

de la nappe atteint 60 m au coeur de cette dépression (Fig. 5.9). C’est une structure hydrogéolo- [SOURCE: Page_214]

gique dont on retrouve des analogues dans plusieurs aquifères du Sahel (Aranyossy and Ndiaye, [SOURCE: Page_214]

1993). La dynamique de ces creux piézométriques reste énigmatique et le flux de sortie per- [SOURCE: Page_214]

mettant leur persistance peu contraint. L’aquifère confiné sous-jacent est artésien dans cette [SOURCE: Page_214]

région, ce qui implique une différence de niveaux piézométriques d’au moins 80 m entre l’aqui- [SOURCE: Page_214]

fère quaternaire et l’aquifère profond. Par conséquent, on peut suspecter un flux de drainance [SOURCE: Page_214]

ascendante. [SOURCE: Page_214]

F IGURE 5.9 – Représentation schématique du bilan d’eau de la dépression piézométrique du Chari- Baguirmi. ET : évapotranspiration ; P : précipitations ; qIV : flux d’eau souterraine dans la nappe quater- naire (au travers de l’isopièze 240 m) ; qD : flux de drainance verticale provenant des aquifères du Pliocene et du Continental Terminal. En régime permanent : qIV +qD = ET-P [SOURCE: Page_214]

Des calculs hydrodynamiques simples permettent d’appréhender le bilan d’eau de la nappe [SOURCE: Page_214]

197 [SOURCE: Page_214]

5. E TUDE DE LA RECHARGE ET DE LA DYNAMIQUE DE L’ AQUIFÈRE QUATERNAIRE À PARTIR DE TRACEURS GÉOCHIMIQUES [SOURCE: Page_215]

phréatique au niveau des dépressions piézométriques. En régime permanent, la somme du flux [SOURCE: Page_215]

qui traverse le coeur de la dépression piézométrique, caractérisé par l’isopièze 240, et du flux de [SOURCE: Page_215]

drainance de l’aquifère profond est égale au bilan entre précipitation (P) et évapotranspiration [SOURCE: Page_215]

(ET) sur la surface de l’isopièze 240 m (Fig. 5.9). [SOURCE: Page_215]

Le flux alimentant le coeur de la dépression peut être calculé par application de la loi de Darcy : [SOURCE: Page_215]

QIV = T × 2πR × ∇h                                       (5.4) QIV q IV = π × R$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_215]

avec T la transmissivité de l’aquifère (m^2 s−1 ), R le rayon de l’isopièze 240 (m), ∇ h le gradient [SOURCE: Page_215]

hydraulique moyen dans la nappe (m m−1 ), QIV le débit volumique (m^3 s−1 ) et qIV la lame [SOURCE: Page_215]

d’eau (m s−1 ) s’écoulant au travers de l’isopièze 240 m. Peu de mesures de transmissivités ont [SOURCE: Page_215]

été réalisées sur l’aquifère quaternaire, en particulier dans sa partie tchadienne (Fig. 1.12). [SOURCE: Page_215]

La transmissivité moyenne mesurée dans le Chari-Baguirmi est de 6x^10−3 m^2 s−1 , le gradient [SOURCE: Page_215]

hydraulique est de 50/150x^10−3 (Massuel, 2001). En régime permanent, le débit qIV s’écoulant [SOURCE: Page_215]

dans l’aquifère et alimentant le coeur de la dépression piézométrique est ainsi estimé à 5 mm [SOURCE: Page_215]

an−1 . [SOURCE: Page_215]

Le flux associé à l’évapotranspiration dans la région a été estimé à partir des données infrarouge [SOURCE: Page_215]

thermique satellitaires (Boronina and Ramillien, 2008) autour de 550 mm an−1 au niveau de la [SOURCE: Page_215]

dépression piézométrique. Avec une précipitation moyenne de l’ordre de 500 mm an−1 dans [SOURCE: Page_215]

cette région, le flux net ET-P est donc de l’ordre de 50 mm an−1 . En considérant en régime [SOURCE: Page_215]

permanent le bilan d’eau dans la nappe phréatique, ceci suggèrerait un flux de drainance de [SOURCE: Page_215]

l’ordre de 45 mm an−1 . Avec un gradient hydraulique entre l’aquifère profond et l’aquifère [SOURCE: Page_215]

quaternaire, de l’ordre de 80/100, le flux de drainance calculé correspond à une perméabilité [SOURCE: Page_215]

des argiles pliocènes de 1.8x^10−9 m s−1 . [SOURCE: Page_215]

Si le flux de drainance est négligé, comme c’est le cas de toutes les études qui ont été réalisées [SOURCE: Page_215]

### 9. Epilogue : une drainance ascendante de l’aquifère profond vers l’aquifère quaternaire ?

sur l’hydrodynamisme de l’aquifère quaternaire, les auteurs calibrent un flux net (ET-P) de [SOURCE: Page_216]

reprise évaporatoire dans la nappe autour de 1 mm an−1 (Leblanc, 2002; Massuel, 2001). Cette [SOURCE: Page_216]

valeur permet, aux incertitudes près, de contrebalancer le flux circulant dans l’aquifère. Par [SOURCE: Page_216]

contre, cette valeur est en désaccord avec les valeurs estimées d’évapotranspiration sur la zone [SOURCE: Page_216]

à partir des mesures infrarouge. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Ce calcul de bilan, réalisé en régime permanent montre qu’hydrodynamiquement, une contri- [SOURCE: Page_216]

bution de l’aquifère profond aux eaux de l’aquifère quaternaire ne peut être exclue. Cependant, [SOURCE: Page_216]

de nombreuses incertitudes jalonnent ce calcul, en particulier la valeur de transmissivité et [SOURCE: Page_216]

l’estimation de ET. Les valeurs de ET sont calculées à partir de mesures ponctuelles tempo- [SOURCE: Page_216]

rellement réalisées sur un mois de saison des pluies (septembre) et un mois de saison sèche [SOURCE: Page_216]

(février) de l’année 2003. A défaut de disposer de plus d’informations hydrodynamiques et [SOURCE: Page_216]

d’une meilleure contrainte des flux ET et P, nous ne pouvons trancher la question. [SOURCE: Page_216]

La caractérisation géochimique de l’aquifère quaternaire a mis en évidence des signatures [SOURCE: Page_216]

particulières des eaux en éléments majeurs, en 36 Cl et en isotopes stables de la molécule d’eau, [SOURCE: Page_216]

en particulier au niveau de la dépression piézométrique. Par conséquent, la caractérisation [SOURCE: Page_216]

géochimique de l’aquifère profond pourrait permettre de mettre en évidence une contribution [SOURCE: Page_216]

des eaux de l’aquifère profond aux eaux de l’aquifère quaternaire. [SOURCE: Page_216]

PARTIR DES DISTRIBUTIONS EN 36 C L 6 Residence time up to 1 Ma of groundwaters in the confined aquifer of the Lake Chad Basin : 36 Cl evidences Bouchez C., Deschamps P., Goncalves J., Hamelin B., Sylvestre F., Doumnang J.C., [SOURCE: Page_218]

### 1. Préambule

1 Préambule [SOURCE: Page_220]

A la fin du chapitre précédent, un moyen proposé pour évaluer si les eaux de l’aquifère qua- [SOURCE: Page_220]

ternaire reçoivent une contribution des eaux des aquifères profonds, est de faire une étude [SOURCE: Page_220]

géochimique de l’aquifère profond. De plus, les eaux souterraines des aquifères profonds du [SOURCE: Page_220]

Tchad forment des ressources en eau souterraine importantes. Celles-ci sont exploitées dans la [SOURCE: Page_220]

zone nigériane du bassin, entrainant une baisse nette des niveaux piézométriques qui menace [SOURCE: Page_220]

l’artésianisme dans cette zone (Goni et al., 2001; Maduabuchi et al., 2006). En revanche, très [SOURCE: Page_220]

peu de forages ont été réalisés dans la partie tchadienne de l’aquifère. [SOURCE: Page_220]

Le but de ce chapitre est donc de contraindre la géochimie de l’aquifère profond dans le but [SOURCE: Page_220]

i) d’identifier sa signature géochimique ii) d’évaluer la dynamique des eaux souterraines pour [SOURCE: Page_220]

mieux appréhender la ressource disponible iii) d’apporter une information paléohydrologique [SOURCE: Page_220]

dans la région. Ce chapitre est une version étendue d’un abstract étendu qui a été accepté (ac- [SOURCE: Page_220]

tuellement en révision mineure) dans le journal Procedia Earth and Planetary Science dans le [SOURCE: Page_220]

cadre de la conférence AIG-11 qui aura lieu en septembre 2015 au BRGM, à Orléans. L’abstract [SOURCE: Page_220]

étendu soumis est présenté dans l’Annexe C. [SOURCE: Page_220]

203 [SOURCE: Page_220]

2. Residence time up to 1Ma of groundwaters in the confined aquifer of the Lake Chad Basin : 36 Cl evidences - Abstract [SOURCE: Page_222]

2 Residence time up to 1Ma of groundwaters in the confined [SOURCE: Page_222]

aquifer of the Lake Chad Basin : 36 Cl evidences - Abstract [SOURCE: Page_222]

Authors : Camille Bouchez, Pierre Deschamps, Julio Goncalves, Bruno Hamelin, Florence [SOURCE: Page_222]

Sylvestre, Jean-Claude Doumnang, Abdallah Mahamat Nour, Ibrahim Baba Goni, Guillaume [SOURCE: Page_222]

Favreau, Pierre Genthon, Jean-Luc Seidel [SOURCE: Page_222]

36 Cl measurements in groundwaters of the deep confined aquifer of the Lake Chad Basin (LCB) [SOURCE: Page_222]

were performed in order to constrain groundwater geochemical ages and residence times. [SOURCE: Page_222]

Twenty-seven wells were sampled in Nigeria, Niger and Chad in the southern parts of the large [SOURCE: Page_222]

(700 000 km^2 ) multi-layered aquifer of the LCB. 36 Cl/Cl values range between 11±1.10−15 to [SOURCE: Page_222]

160±8.10−15 at at−1 . The highest ratios are observed near the recharge zone of the Nigerian [SOURCE: Page_222]

part of the Continental Terminal aquifer and at the Pays-Bas discharge zone. The lowest ratios [SOURCE: Page_222]

are found in wells located near the southern fringe of the present-day lake Chad. Chloride [SOURCE: Page_222]

concentrations are low (below 120 mg L−1 ) and not correlated to the 36 Cl/Cl values, indicating [SOURCE: Page_222]

negligible dissolution of evaporites in most samples. Reliable 36 Cl ages can be calculated along [SOURCE: Page_222]

the different flow paths investigated, suggesting residence times of the deep groundwaters [SOURCE: Page_222]

larger than 300 000 years and up to 1Ma in the central part of the aquifer. These results are [SOURCE: Page_222]

consistent with new AMS-14 C data below the detection limit but are in contradiction with [SOURCE: Page_222]

previous 14 C data obtained in the area. [SOURCE: Page_222]

Key words : Lake Chad Basin ; 36 Cl -ages ; groundwater resources [SOURCE: Page_222]

3 Introduction [SOURCE: Page_222]

Large sedimentary aquifers of northern and western Africa contain important groundwater re- [SOURCE: Page_222]

sources (MacDonald et al., 2012). In these semi-arid to arid regions, their use is expected to in- [SOURCE: Page_222]

crease substantially to combat growing food insecurity. Improved knowledge of the hydrological [SOURCE: Page_222]

behavior and recharge of these aquifers is essential to properly assess availability and sustai- [SOURCE: Page_222]

205 [SOURCE: Page_222]

6. T EMPS DE RÉSIDENCE DANS LES EAUX DES AQUIFÈRES CONFINÉS DU LCB À PARTIR DES DISTRIBUTIONS EN 36 C L [SOURCE: Page_223]

nability of these resources (Scanlon et al., 2006). This is specially the case for the multi-layer [SOURCE: Page_223]

aquifer system of the Lake Chad Basin (LCB) that remains poorly studied. Located in Central [SOURCE: Page_223]

Africa, at the transition between Sahel and Sahara, the LCB is one of the greatest endorheic ba- [SOURCE: Page_223]

sins of the world. It encloses a multi-layered aquifer composed of two main aquifer units : an [SOURCE: Page_223]

unconfined Quaternary aquifer and a deeper confined aquifer. The latter is subdivided into Lo- [SOURCE: Page_223]

wer (Continental Terminal formation) and Middle (early Pliocene formation) aquifer sub-units [SOURCE: Page_223]

in the Nigerian part of the LCB (Edmunds et al., 1999; Maduabuchi et al., 2006). For the sake of [SOURCE: Page_223]

simplicity, they are undifferentiated in this study and both named Continental Terminal (CT). [SOURCE: Page_223]

The CT is mostly artesian, but increasing withdrawal in the populated area of northeastern Ni- [SOURCE: Page_223]

geria leads to a significant pressure decline (Goni et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_223]

According to a comprehensive review of the rare hydrogeological study carried out on the [SOURCE: Page_223]

Continental Terminal system (Schneider and Wolff, 1992; Schneider, 2004), the waters flow from [SOURCE: Page_223]

the South West of the Basin (present-day recharge zone) to the North East depression of the [SOURCE: Page_223]

“Bas-Pays” (present-day discharge zone), Fig. 6.1. Geochemical investigations in the Nigerian [SOURCE: Page_223]

part of the deep aquifers provide 14 C-dated varying between 20 or 40 ka (Edmunds et al., 1999; [SOURCE: Page_223]

Maduabuchi et al., 2006). These apparent 14 C groundwater ages raise several issues. First, 14 C [SOURCE: Page_223]

contents are within the range between 0 and 10 pmc where the classical techniques may be [SOURCE: Page_223]

significantly biased and 14 C contents sould be re-evaluated (Aggarwal et al., 2014). Indeed, the [SOURCE: Page_223]

uptake of atmospheric CO$_2$ during sample collection and analysis induces consequent lowering [SOURCE: Page_223]

of estimated ages, particularly at low 14 C contents (Aggarwal et al., 2014). Second, massive [SOURCE: Page_223]

recharge periods of the Continental Terminal between 20 and 40 ka suggest humid conditions [SOURCE: Page_223]

in the Sahel at that time (Edmunds et al., 1999; Gasse, 2000). Paleoclimatic reconstructions [SOURCE: Page_223]

based both on continental sedimental records (Garcin et al., 2006) and oceanic sedimental [SOURCE: Page_223]

records (Lezine and Cazet, 2005; Tierney and deMenocal, 2013; Tjallingii et al., 2008) all indicate [SOURCE: Page_223]

arid conditions in Sahel during the last glaciation (Gasse, 2000; Hoelzmann et al., 2004). The [SOURCE: Page_223]

age determination of groundwater of the confined aquifers is central in this paleoclimatic [SOURCE: Page_223]

controversy. [SOURCE: Page_223]

206 [SOURCE: Page_223]

### 3. Introduction

In this study, we propose to use, alternatively to 14 C, another cosmogenic nuclide, the chlorine- [SOURCE: Page_224]

36 to provide further constraints on groundwater dynamic in a large part of the Continental [SOURCE: Page_224]

Terminal aquifer that extends over Niger, Nigeria and Chad. Chlorine-36 is a powerful tool in [SOURCE: Page_224]

hydrological studies and in groundwater dating because of the conservative behavior of chorine [SOURCE: Page_224]

and its half-life of 3x^105 a−1 . Although this tool was largely applied in many large aquifer systems [SOURCE: Page_224]

around the world (Phillips, 2000), it is the first time that 36 Cl analyses were performed on LCB [SOURCE: Page_224]

aquifers. [SOURCE: Page_224]

F IGURE 6.1 – Study site : The Continental Terminal of the Lake Chad Basin. Localization of the Lake Chad Basin in Africa ; Hydrogeological map of the Continental Terminal ; Position of groundwater samples ; Hydrogeological cross-section of the Continental Terminal (d’après Schneider and Wolff (1992)) [SOURCE: Page_224]

6. T EMPS DE RÉSIDENCE DANS LES EAUX DES AQUIFÈRES CONFINÉS DU LCB À PARTIR DES DISTRIBUTIONS EN 36 C L [SOURCE: Page_225]

4 Samples and Methods [SOURCE: Page_225]

Groundwaters of thirty-three wells from the Continental Terminal aquifer were sampled in [SOURCE: Page_225]

the Nigeria, Niger and Chad parts of the Basin from 2008 to 2013 (Fig. 6.1). On site analysis [SOURCE: Page_225]

included temperature, electrical conductivity, dissolved oxygen concentration, pH and alka- [SOURCE: Page_225]

linity titration. The depth of water abstraction is unknown. However, according to the mean [SOURCE: Page_225]

geothermal gradient of 3◦ C per 100 m evidenced in this area (Maduabuchi et al., 2006), depth [SOURCE: Page_225]

of our groundwater samples ranges between 0 and 500 m. Water samples can be divided in five [SOURCE: Page_225]

groups according to their geographic location (Fig. 6.1). Two samples (#4) are located close to [SOURCE: Page_225]

the recharge zone and five samples (#5) are located in the Bas-Pays, at the discharge zone of [SOURCE: Page_225]

the Continental Terminal (Fig. 6.1). Their temperature between 30 and 33 ◦ C indicates shallow [SOURCE: Page_225]

groundwater between 0 and 100m depth. Samples in the center of the aquifer align along three [SOURCE: Page_225]

different groundwater flowpaths (#1, #2, #3, Fig. 6.1), with water depth between 100 and 500m [SOURCE: Page_225]

## (34<T(◦ C)<45).

Major and minor ion analyses were carried out on filtered aliquots by ion chromatography (IC) [SOURCE: Page_225]

at HydroSciences Montpellier. The isotopic characterization of the groundwaters of the Conti- [SOURCE: Page_225]

nental Terminal includes 36 Cl, 14 C and water stable isotopes. 36 Cl measurements were carried [SOURCE: Page_225]

out at the French AMS National Facility ASTER (Elmore et al., 1979), following the procedure [SOURCE: Page_225]

described in Phillips (2000). No spike was added to the samples to avoid 36 Cl spike contribu- [SOURCE: Page_225]

tion. All samples have 36 Cl/Cl ratios at least ten times above the chemical blank ratio (5-10.10−16 [SOURCE: Page_225]

at at−1 ) with a reproducibility of 8 % (Bouchez et al., 2015b). 14 C-AMS analyses (Artemis-AMS [SOURCE: Page_225]

facility) were performed on four samples originating from the Chadian zone, following the pro- [SOURCE: Page_225]

cedure described in Delqué-Količ et al. (2013). Because of the very low 14 C contents measured [SOURCE: Page_225]

in previous studies (<10pmc, Edmunds et al. (1999); Maduabuchi et al. (2006)), special care was [SOURCE: Page_225]

taken to avoid contamination during 14 C-dedicated water sampling and inox bottles with two [SOURCE: Page_225]

entries were used. This device allows flushing air outside the bottle and ensuring no contact [SOURCE: Page_225]

with the atmosphere. The 18 O and 2 H composition of water samples have been measured using [SOURCE: Page_225]

an IRMS, at CEREGE. [SOURCE: Page_225]

208 [SOURCE: Page_225]

### 5. Results

5 Results [SOURCE: Page_226]

All groundwaters are Na-enriched, either of Na-HCO$_3$ or Na-Cl-SO$_4$ type waters, corresponding [SOURCE: Page_226]

to evolved groundwaters significantly different from present-day surface waters (Chap.5, 5.2). [SOURCE: Page_226]

The chloride concentrations range between 2 and 117 mg L−1 (Table 6.1). [SOURCE: Page_226]

36 Cl/Cl values (>100.10−15 at at−1 ) and very low chloride content waters (below 6 mg L−1 ) are [SOURCE: Page_227]

from samples located near the nigerian recharge zone (#4) and the Bas-Pays discharge zone [SOURCE: Page_227]

(#5). The lowest 36 Cl/Cl ratios (around 10 to 15.10−15 at at−1 , n=7) are measured in groundwater [SOURCE: Page_227]

samples located in Chad and the eastern part of Nigeria (#1, #2, #3). The four 14 C analyses are [SOURCE: Page_227]

below the detection limit of Artemis-AMS facility (< 0.5 pmC). [SOURCE: Page_227]

The stable isotopic compositions, δ18 O and δ2 H show values in a narrow range between -5 and [SOURCE: Page_227]

-7 ‰ and -38 and -57 ‰ respectively (Table 6.2). Water samples from the Nigerien and Chadian [SOURCE: Page_227]

6 Discussion [SOURCE: Page_229]

6.1 Characterization of paleorecharge of the CT [SOURCE: Page_229]

The stable isotopic compositions of groundwaters of the deep confined aquifer show waters de- [SOURCE: Page_229]

pleted in heavy isotopes when compared to waters of the Quaternary Aquifer (δ18 O between [SOURCE: Page_229]

-5 and 6 ‰) and modern precipitation (δ18 O weighted average of -3.8 ‰) evidencing paleo- [SOURCE: Page_229]

recharge water. A depleted signature in the groundwaters can be induced at first order by i) [SOURCE: Page_229]

decrease of average air temperature (temperature effect) ii) increased distance from the coast [SOURCE: Page_229]

(continental effect) and iii) increased precipitation (amount effect), (Beyerle et al., 2003; Ro- [SOURCE: Page_229]

zanski et al., 1993). At tropical latitudes, there is no straightforward dependence between air [SOURCE: Page_229]

temperature and stable isotopes in precipitation (Rozanski et al., 1993) and because of conside- [SOURCE: Page_229]

rable recycling of continental water, the gradient of continentality in the region is weak (Joseph [SOURCE: Page_229]

et al., 1992; Taupin et al., 2000). On the other hand, the correlation between monthly δ18 O va- [SOURCE: Page_229]

lues and the amount of rain is well recognized in Sahel (Goni et al., 2001; Taupin et al., 2000) [SOURCE: Page_229]

and a gradient of -1 to -2 ‰ per 100 mm was estimated (Fontes et al., 1993). Therefore, the [SOURCE: Page_229]

amount effect is the most reliable explanation of the difference between the mean δ18 O of CT [SOURCE: Page_229]

groundwaters of -6.2 ‰ and the weighted mean average of present-day precipitation of -3.8 ‰. [SOURCE: Page_229]

It would correspond to an increase of annual rainfall of about 200 mm which could be related [SOURCE: Page_229]

to an increased frequency of heavy rains events associated with an intensification of northward [SOURCE: Page_229]

movement of the ITCZ (Beyerle et al., 2003; Fontes et al., 1993). A shift in the origin of air mass [SOURCE: Page_229]

could also explain the difference in the stable isotopic composition. [SOURCE: Page_229]

### 6. Discussion

6.2 Origin of chlorine in the groundwaters of the CT ? [SOURCE: Page_230]

Low chloride concentrations together with the absence of correlation between the 36 Cl/Cl and [SOURCE: Page_230]

[Cl− ] suggest no addition of dead chlorine in most samples. The Cl/Br molar ratios measured in [SOURCE: Page_230]

groundwaters (Table 6.1) are below the seawater ratio (630, Gerritse and George (1988)) and in [SOURCE: Page_230]

the range of Cl/Br ratios measured in rainfall in northern Nigeria (between 200 and 400, Goni [SOURCE: Page_230]

et al. (2001)). None of the groundwater displays the very high Cl/Br ratios, indicative of signi- [SOURCE: Page_230]

ficant evaporite dissolution. Moreover, there is a trend toward more 18 O-enriched water as Cl [SOURCE: Page_230]

increases in concentration (Fig. 6.3). Therefore, Cl concentrations in groundwaters of the CT are [SOURCE: Page_230]

interpreted in terms of evaporative processes and dissolution of evaporite is neglected. [SOURCE: Page_230]

36 6.3         Cl/Cl initial ratio [SOURCE: Page_230]

The highest measured values in the CT are around 150x^10−15 (# 4,5). This value is however [SOURCE: Page_230]

significantly lower that the 36 Cl/Cl signature of the Holocene groundwaters of the Chadian part [SOURCE: Page_230]

of the Quaternary aquifer that are characterized by a homogeneous pre-anthropic initial 36 Cl/Cl [SOURCE: Page_230]

ratio of 216x^10−15 at at−1 (Bouchez et al., 2014). This significant discrepancy can be interpreted [SOURCE: Page_230]

either by 1) age effect or by 2) a different initial ratio in groudwaters if the CT compared to [SOURCE: Page_230]

the initial ratio measured in the Quaternary aquifer. This difference in "initial" ratio might [SOURCE: Page_230]

be explained by spatial and temporal variations in 36 Cl fallout and/or chloride content in [SOURCE: Page_230]

precipitation (Davis et al., 2003; Plummer et al., 1997) over the LCB. [SOURCE: Page_230]

The choice of the 36 Cl/Cl initial ratio is pivotal as it is the primary source of uncertainties in the [SOURCE: Page_230]

determination of 36 Cl-derived ages (Davis et al., 1998). The age effect would indicate waters in [SOURCE: Page_230]

the near vicinity of the Nigerian CT outcrop of about 120 000 years. Therefore, using either 200 [SOURCE: Page_230]

or 150 x^10−15 at at−1 as the initial ratio leads to a shift in groundwater ages of 120 000 years. [SOURCE: Page_230]

6.4 Groundwater ages in the CT [SOURCE: Page_230]

The equilibrium ratio found in sandstone formations similar to the Chadian formations is [SOURCE: Page_230]

usually below 10.10−15 at at−1 (Fabryka-Martin, 1988). All 36 Cl/Cl ratios in the CT are over [SOURCE: Page_230]

213 [SOURCE: Page_230]

6. T EMPS DE RÉSIDENCE DANS LES EAUX DES AQUIFÈRES CONFINÉS DU LCB À PARTIR DES DISTRIBUTIONS EN 36 C L [SOURCE: Page_231]

this value. Although this requires complementary investigation, we hypothesize that these [SOURCE: Page_231]

waters have not reached the secular equilibrium and hypogenic production can be neglected. [SOURCE: Page_231]

Therefore the general decrease trend in both 36 Cl/Cl and 36 Cl observed along the two flow paths [SOURCE: Page_231]

#1 and #2 (green and black points, Fig. 6.2) can be interpreted at face value as 36 Cl radioactive [SOURCE: Page_231]

decay. The northern transect (#1) evidences higher chlorine concentration at recharge than [SOURCE: Page_231]

the western transect (#2). This is supported by the different water sources inferred from stable [SOURCE: Page_231]

isotopic composition and suggests increasing evaporation in the northern part of the basin. [SOURCE: Page_231]

Assuming the initial 36 Cl/Cl ratio of 150x^10−15 at at−1 , 36 Cl ages can be estimated and lead to [SOURCE: Page_231]

groundwaters older than 300 000 years for most of the samples in the central part of the aquifer [SOURCE: Page_231]

(with the exception of 3 samples of the flow path #2 that are likely younger, Table 6.2). For the [SOURCE: Page_231]

samples characterized by 36 Cl/Cl ranging from 10 to 20.10−15 at at−1 and situated at the end of [SOURCE: Page_231]

the three flow paths (#1,2,3), 36 Cl ages attain 1 Ma. The distribution of calculated ages shows an [SOURCE: Page_231]

unexpected feature as the waters at the discharge zone of the CT are found to be younger that [SOURCE: Page_231]

waters at the center of the CT. [SOURCE: Page_231]

These results are highly consistent with the four 14 C analyses of groundwater samples from the [SOURCE: Page_231]

central part of the aquifer, that are below the detection limit of Artemis-AMS facility (< 0.5 pmC). [SOURCE: Page_231]

With the limitation of the small number of 14 C-analyse samples, these data clearly indicate ages [SOURCE: Page_231]

older than 50 ka, in agreement with 36 Cl-derived ages. These results, in contradiction with pre- [SOURCE: Page_231]

vious studies (Edmunds et al., 1999; Maduabuchi et al., 2006), give new insights to the paleohy- [SOURCE: Page_231]

drology of the survey area. It also confirms that special care is highly needed in the sampling of [SOURCE: Page_231]

waters for 14 C measurements (Aggarwal et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_231]

6.5 Groundwater dynamics in the CT [SOURCE: Page_231]

The transit time from the recharge area (#4) to the center of the aquifer where the oldest waters [SOURCE: Page_231]

are found (#2,3) can be estimated from the application of the Darcy law. The transmissivity of [SOURCE: Page_231]

the aquifer is estimated between 1 and 4x^10−4 m^2 s−1 (Maduabuchi et al., 2006) and a mean [SOURCE: Page_231]

### 7. Conclusion

thickness of 100 m is considered (Maduabuchi et al., 2006). The hydraulic gradient of 0.015 [SOURCE: Page_232]

% estimated for the Pliocene aquifer (Maduabuchi et al., 2006) is consistent with the mean [SOURCE: Page_232]

hydraulic gradient between the recharge area (h=340) and the discharge area (h=260) that [SOURCE: Page_232]

are separated by 1000 km. By using an effective porosity of 5 %, corresponding to the value [SOURCE: Page_232]

estimated in the Continental Terminal in Niger (Favreau, 2000), a simple calculation leads to [SOURCE: Page_232]

a transit time between 80 and 350 ka. The calculated transit time is at first order inconsistent [SOURCE: Page_232]

with water ages up to 1 Ma in the center of the aquifer. [SOURCE: Page_232]

The formation of MegaLake Chad was considered to have been responsible for closing the [SOURCE: Page_232]

groundwater discharge outlet (Edmunds et al., 1998) during the African Humid Period. The [SOURCE: Page_232]

extensive lake, filling the Bas Pays, reaches an altitude of 325 m (Schuster et al., 2005). The [SOURCE: Page_232]

difference of piezometric heads at the discharge area between a present-day lake Chad and a [SOURCE: Page_232]

MegaLake Chad stages thus accounts for 65 m. Although MegaLake Chad phases are episodic [SOURCE: Page_232]

(Schuster et al., 2006), they could induce a strong decrease of the hydraulic gradient and of [SOURCE: Page_232]

flow velocities. The alternanating of humid and arid periods related to interglacial and glacial [SOURCE: Page_232]

periods deeply affect recharge rates and flow velocities (Petersen et al., 2014). The occurrence [SOURCE: Page_232]

of young waters at the discharge zone would require an inversion of the hydraulic gradient, [SOURCE: Page_232]

that remains so far elusive. The investigation of the groundwater dynamics in response to the [SOURCE: Page_232]

subsequent changes in the boundary conditions both at recharge and discharge areas could [SOURCE: Page_232]

be performed from a hydrogeological modeling. Our 36 Cl data provide essential information to [SOURCE: Page_232]

constrain groundwater flows in the CT. [SOURCE: Page_232]

7 Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_232]

36 Cl is a suitable tracer for groundwater dating in the deep confined aquifer of the Lake Chad [SOURCE: Page_232]

Basin because of low chloride content of groundwaters compared to other large aquifers of nor- [SOURCE: Page_232]

thern Africa. Its application provides robust evidence that groundwater residence times are su- [SOURCE: Page_232]

perior to 300 ka. Recent waters are not clearly evidenced even close to the recharge zone, indi- [SOURCE: Page_232]

cating very limited if any active recharge of the system. These first results are pivotal for sustai- [SOURCE: Page_232]

215 [SOURCE: Page_232]

6. T EMPS DE RÉSIDENCE DANS LES EAUX DES AQUIFÈRES CONFINÉS DU LCB À PARTIR DES DISTRIBUTIONS EN 36 C L [SOURCE: Page_233]

nable water management of groundwater resources in the Lake Chad Basin. [SOURCE: Page_233]

This study provides an other example of strong discrepancy between 36 Cl-derived ages and 14 C- [SOURCE: Page_233]

derived ages based on old analysis. This suggests that 14 C data should be interpreted with spe- [SOURCE: Page_233]

cial care when values lie below 10 pmc (Aggarwal et al., 2014). Finally, our study provides in- [SOURCE: Page_233]

teresting insight of paleoclimatic reconstruction in the Sahelian belt and in particular does not [SOURCE: Page_233]

support massive recharge period between 20 and 40 ka. [SOURCE: Page_233]

8 Epilogue [SOURCE: Page_233]

Les différences géochimiques nettes qui ont été mises en évidence entre les deux aquifères ne [SOURCE: Page_233]

vont pas dans le sens d’un flux de drainance ascendant. D’un point de vue géochimique, les [SOURCE: Page_233]

deux aquifères sont bien distincts. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Conclusion générale et perspectives [SOURCE: Page_234]

Le Bassin du lac Tchad (BLT), qui couvre 2.5 millions de km^2 au coeur de la zone sahélienne, [SOURCE: Page_234]

illustre la plupart des enjeux liés aux ressources en eau dans cette zone : une vulnérabilité [SOURCE: Page_234]

des eaux de surface qui contraste avec des ressources présumées importantes en eau souter- [SOURCE: Page_234]

raine, bien que trop peu étudiées. L’objectif central de ce travail était d’évaluer la réponse hy- [SOURCE: Page_234]

dro(géo)logique du BLT aux variations climatiques. Nous revenons ici sur les questions ciblées [SOURCE: Page_234]

à l’issue du chapitre d’état de l’art. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Principaux résultats de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_234]

Fonctionnement hydrologique du bassin versant du Chari-Logone [SOURCE: Page_234]

Les transferts à l’échelle du bassin versant sont indirectement étudiés dans cette étude. La [SOURCE: Page_234]

signature en 36 Cl des bombes dans les rivières atteste d’un temps de transfert non négligeable [SOURCE: Page_234]

de l’eau dans la partie amont du bassin versant du Chari-Logone. Sur la base de notre étude, [SOURCE: Page_234]

un temps de résidence de 10 ans des eaux souterraines qui participent au débit de base du [SOURCE: Page_234]

Chari-Logone est estimé. Le modèle hydrologique GR2M est un autre moyen, quoique concep- [SOURCE: Page_234]

tuel, d’appréhender les temps de transfert dans le bassin. Appliqué au bassin du Chari-Logone, [SOURCE: Page_234]

l’écoulement "instantané" dans GR2M est nul et le paramètre X$_2$ est faible, ce qui indique que [SOURCE: Page_234]

le bassin réagit lentement. Ces deux approches vont dans le sens d’un temps de transfert non [SOURCE: Page_234]

négligeable de l’eau dans le bassin versant du Chari-Logone. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Fonctionnement hydrologique et géochimique du lac Tchad sur les dernières décennies [SOURCE: Page_234]

Le fonctionnement hydrologique et géochimique du lac Tchad a été abordé à l’aide de bilans [SOURCE: Page_234]
